#6936. A Crash Course in Corduroy

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

It’s the perfect time of year for a tough, warm fabric with tons of texture. Here’s how to work it into your wardrobe.

According to a recent and deeply unscientific poll conducted by yours truly, some guys are confounded by corduroy. They like it. They want to wear it. But they’re not quite sure how it figures into their wardrobes. This is a pity, because corduroy is great. It’s warm, it’s hard-wearing, it delivers texture galore, and it can work beautifully in the context of all different types of personal style.

So in the interest of getting these guys—and you!—properly outfitted, I’m offering this crash course in corduroy. It’ll give you the key information you need in order to understand the fabric’s history and how you can wear it now. Let’s dive in.

There’s a great story about the name corduroy being derived from the French phrase “corde du roi,” or “cord of the king.” It conjures up all sorts of noble associations and sounds cool, like so much menswear lore does. The problem is, it’s likely apocryphal. No one really knows the root of the word for sure. (But if you want to bust out that tidbit at a dinner party and let your friends believe it, we wouldn’t blame you.)

What we do know is that corduroy traces its roots back to a category of fabric called fustian—basically heavy cloth, often used for menswear, that was usually woven from cotton (though there have been woolen and linen variations over the many centuries that fustian has existed). The name might come from the city of Fustat, near Cairo, which manufactured that sort of cloth. Or maybe it doesn’t. More menswear lore!

What sets corduroy apart from other fustian cloths like velveteen and moleskin is its distinctive ribbing. That innovation is newer; it was developed in Manchester around the middle of the 18th century, when the city became a center of production for cotton fabrics like velvet and the modern iteration of corduroy. The ribs are known as wales, and they’re created by weaving extra thread into the base fabric and then trimming them to created tufted, vertical ridges offset by channels between them.

This “modern” corduroy quickly became a workwear staple thanks to its durability and warmth. It was only later that it migrated to British country clothing and, eventually, to the rest of the globe. It slowly filtered into U.S. academia throughout the first half of the 20th century, eventually leading to the well-known stereotype of the college professor with the well-worn and elbow-patched corduroy jacket. It hit the counterculture in the ’60s. And it absolutely boomed in the ’70s, showing up on the era’s leading men both on and off the silver screen.

Since then, as with all menswear staples, its popularity has ebbed and flowed. But like denim, it never truly goes out of style. It’s all about how you wear it.

The width of the wales is measured by how many you can fit into an inch, so an 8-wale corduroy has thicker ridges than, say, a 12-wale corduroy. Most “standard” corduroy sits around 11 to 13 wales per inch. Chunkier stuff has a lower number, while “pinwale” or “needlecord” corduroy is higher, around 16 or above. As you might guess, the chunkier stuff reads more rugged and the finer stuff feels more refined.

Developed in and named after the town of New Bedford, Massachusetts, this fabric has distinctive vertical ridges, loads of durability, and roots in the world of workwear. It’s not quite corduroy, though. The ridges are created in the way the base fabric is woven, not by adding additional threads and trimming them. Still, it’s worth having on your radar if you like the look and feel of corduroy.

Just … wear it. I know that sounds like fake advice, but it’s really not worth overthinking it. Yes, you’ll want to think about the weather, because corduroy can wear warmer than other fabrics. And you might want to get yourself used to the idea of the occasional whoosh of the ribbed fabric rubbing against itself. (It’s not as distracting as the crinkle of a nylon shell suit, but it’s audible.) But corduroy is in its current form because it works. It wears in beautifully, fends off the cold, and adds some always welcome depth and texture to any outfit.

If you’re still nervous, start with something simple, like a corduroy jacket—your call on whether it’s a sport coat or something more utilitarian—or some five-pocket cord pants. You could rock it as an overshirt (go wide wale) or as a button-up. You could even channel everyone from Wes Anderson to Robert Redford with a full corduroy suit, though you might want to avoid the elbow patches if you don’t want to look too professorial.

As for what to wear it with? Well, nothing too techy. A super-stretchy golf shirt doesn’t exactly match the vibe; ditto for polyester joggers. And nothing too fine or fancy, either. Because of its more casual and countryish vibe, corduroy might not be the best complement to spit-polished oxford shoes or a particularly delicate worsted wool. The idea is to look for a sense of cohesion with your outfit, and anything that reads as deliberately futuristic or self-consciously dressy could detract from that.

Otherwise, options abound, especially when you start talking about other classic menswear fabrics and items. Corduroy looks great with beefy oxford-cloth button-downs. It plays well with denim and chino and flannel and hopsack. And don’t be afraid of going all in on texture. Bust out a shaggy Shetland or cable-knit sweater. Bring some tweed into the mix. Corduroy shouldn’t be confusing; it should be easy. So play around a little. You’re bound to have a (sorry) wale of a good time.

#6934. Victoria Beckham’s Fiery “Chili-Pepper Nails” Are Winter’s Most Expensive-Looking Manicure

Traditional red nails just got a hot upgrade for the colder months

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Victoria Beckham has many signature looks, but when it comes to her nails, she’s almost always sporting a neutral, minimalist pink manicure or a subtle French manicure; they’re practically a part of her uniform, and her fingertips are rarely painted with bright colors. However, in her latest Instagram post, Beckham’s signature manicure was nowhere to be seen. Instead, the fashion designer opted for elegant, chili-pepper red fingertips.

The fiery red hue marked a significant departure from her trademark look, but it still read equally sophisticated. As Beckham showed, red nails are classic for a reason: confident, polished, and endlessly versatile. Even in fashion, red has the power to elevate an entire outfit. While bright red isn’t everyone’s cup of tea (Posh Spice possibly included), there are a variety of red hues that are more subtle but still carry the power.

It’s a sentiment echoed by nail artist Jessica White, who previously told Bazaar, “A clean red manicure is timeless because it’s powerful, but understated at the same time. It doesn’t need embellishment—it’s a statement on its own.”

As the perfect antidote to the incoming winter’s dark, damp nights, it’s the perfect time to take inspiration from Beckham and embrace a simple but bold refresh. The best part: Red is foolproof and works no matter the shape or length of your nails and always feels fashion-forward. After all, Beckham wouldn’t co-sign it otherwise.

#6933. 23 of Heidi Klum’s Most Iconic Halloween Costumes Through the Years

Her severed hand costume is just the latest jaw-dropping one in her history.

No one does Halloween quite like Heidi Klum. Since 2000, the supermodel has been throwing yearly Halloween parties where celebs gather to mingle and have fun. Just as exciting as her annual bashes, however, are the truly over-the-top costumes she dreams up. Each year, Klum arrives in a costume more elaborate and incredible than the last—it’s truly a sight to see.

Ahead of Halloween, we took a walk down memory lane and rounded up some of the legend’s most memorable costume ensembles throughout the years. From adorable recreations of cartoon characters to fantastical superhero outfits, here are some of our favorites.

For her first-ever Halloween party, Klum wore a striking leather outfit that included a black wig, spiked collar, and knee-high boots. Her red lips and voluminous lashes were also nice touches.

The supermodel pulled a Bianca Jagger and arrived at the function in 2001 dressed up as the 13th-century horse-riding legend Lady Godiva. Her all-white ensemble featured a silver sequined bodysuit paired with a long bleached blonde wig.

The stunning look was created by designer Marc Bouwer. Here, Klum smiles during a fitting for the creation.

In 2002, the star decided on an adorable Betty Boop costume with a strappy red dress, red stilettos, elbow-length gloves, and suitably extravagant makeup. Her Halloween party that year was held at Capitale restaurant in New York City.

She’s pictured here posing with her fellow Victoria’s Secret Angel, Karolina Kurkova, while at the festivities.

For her 2003 holiday fête, the supermodel chose a gold alienesque look which showcased a metallic headpiece and gold metal teeth.

Klum showed up to her bash dressed in a witchy red look that consisted of a waist-length red wig, voluminous pointed hat, bone accessories, and plenty of red eye makeup. The party was held at Marquee in New York City that year.

She wore another spooky look for her 2005 Halloween get-together held in Happy Valley, New York. It’s hard to recognize her between the gigantic black wig and the pointy fangs.

The model attended her 7th annual Halloween party dressed as the forbidden apple (snake headdress and apple dress included) from the biblical story of Adam and Eve. Her then-husband, Seal, showed up as Eve.

In 2007, she stole attention at her get-together dressed in a head-to-toe cat ensemble which consisted of a gray leotard, bedazzled collar, and lifelike whiskers. The party kicked off in Los Angeles that year.

She’s pictured here testing the look a few days before the festivities.

A few years later, Klum was photographed on the red carpet walking into her event dressed as a superhero. The super original look showcased towering platform shoes, an elbow-length red wig, and all-over purple sparkles.

Klum shocked everyone when she opted to wear a skintight costume that transformed her into a skinless body. To make the look even more convincing, she was wheeled onto the red carpet on a stretcher with a “surgeon” by her side.

She flashed a smile at the camera while getting fitted a month or so before the event.

For her 13th Halloween party, Klum had a team of designers custom make her Cleopatra costume. She offered fans a sneak peek ahead of the finished product.

Klum decided to travel into the future and show up to her 2013 Halloween celebration dressed up as a much older version of herself. She donned a gray wig, a walking cane, and a strand of pearls for the occasion.

Klum wowed partygoers when she chose an intricate and hyper-realistic butterfly costume for her 2014 event.

Here’s a closer look at her awesome face paint that year.

The supermodel pulled out all the stops when she arrived at her 2015 party wearing a Jessica Rabbit costume. Between the lacy red dress, purple gloves, and makeup, Klum looks just like the iconic character.

Klum worked with talented makeup artists to bring her look to the next level.

#6930. Kendall Jenner Recreated a Cult-Favorite ’80s Movie Look for Halloween

With a November 3 birthday, Kendall Jenner started the celebrations early. The model threw a party on October 30 to fête Halloween and turning 31.

On October 31, she shared photos of her costume. The model transformed into Jane Henderson, the character played by Nastassja Kinski in Wim Wenders’s 1984 film Paris, Texas. The model traded her signature brunette hair for a chin-length platinum-blonde bob. She paired the look with a fuzzy, low-back hot pink minidress and sheer black tights. In one photo, she channels a memorable scene from the film, posing at a bar.

For those unfamiliar with the reference, Paris, Texas follows Travis Henderson, a mysterious drifter who reappears after four years away and attempts to reconnect with his young son and estranged wife, Jane. Directed by Wenders and written by Pulitzer Prize-winning playwright Sam Shepard, the film is considered one of the German filmmaker’s most celebrated works. It took home the Palme d’Or at the 1984 Cannes Film Festival and has since earned cult film status. More than four decades later, its imagery remains recognizable to cinephiles and fashion fans alike.

Jenner wasn’t alone in her cinematic costume. Actor Jacob Elordi joined her as Travis Henderson, the character portrayed by Harry Dean Stanton in the film.

While it marks the rumored couple’s first costume together, it’s not the first year Jenner has thrown a birthday party during Halloween weekend. In 2019, she teased that she was “keeping the Halloween birthday party alive for mom” on Instagram.

This year’s bash, which was thrown at Bar Marmont in Los Angeles, had a star-studded guest list. Attendees included Charli XCX, Hailey Bieber, Laura Harrier, and more.

#6925. This “Cool Girl” British Lighting Brand Is Sold on Amazon, and I Just Bought Its Cutest Lamp

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

When it comes to buying home decor, I follow a strict “bougie on a budget” philosophy. Sure, I love to fill my space with charming, elevated items, but I’m not exactly willing to spend a small fortune to outfit my rental apartment. So you can imagine my sheer surprise and delight when I learned you can buy a lamp from Pooky Lighting right on Amazon.

If you know, you know, but just in case you don’t? The British brand has been the cool girl’s secret to great lighting ever since it crossed the pond in 2024. Pooky might sell every fixture imaginable—and even have collaborations with Novogratz and Morris &. Co.—but its pièce de résistance is the portable, rechargeable lights.

A whimsical glow that goes wherever you go? Sold...well, if they weren’t so expensive. A quick scroll shows prices starting at $115 and easily going above $200. Not exactly conducive to my “bougie on a budget” mindset. When I stumbled upon two Pooky portable lights for $100 on Amazon, I thought it was a mistake or a very convincing knockoff. However, upon further inspection, these lights seem legit. Though both lights have the same lower price tag, they have two completely different looks. With a pleated shade and bulbous base, the Cecily lamp will pair nicely with midcentury-inspired or even contemporary spaces. If, like me, you are looking for something more aligned with Pooky’s English roots, might I suggest the Primrose style? The woven shade and scalloped details fit perfectly with my apartment’s traditional coastal vibe. Both options come in seven colorways, so you’re bound to find one that matches your interiors.

As soon as my lamp arrived, I charged the Genesis Cell light source, which can reportedly hold a charge for up to 17 hours, and immediately placed it on my kitchen counter. I always joke I’m allergic to harsh, overhead lighting, so I love how this fixture illuminates my culinary space without making the entire room obnoxiously, blindingly bright. And, since it's cordless, I can really take it anywhere. Next to my oven when I cook? Above the dishwasher when I’m only washing plates and glassware? Near the coffee machine to make my morning brew? All of the above, and then some.

But perhaps my favorite detail is the accompanying remote control, which has a timer setting. It’s all too easy to turn on a portable lamp, forget about it, and wind up with zero battery the next time you need a light. With this fixture, I can light my kitchen for 15, 30, or 60 minutes and have it magically turn off on its own. Now that’s a bright idea.

#6915. Why Taylor Swift Hasn’t Been Seen at Travis Kelce’s Chiefs-Broncos Game in Denver

Taylor Swift has attended all of Travis Kelce’s home games this year, but during the Kansas City Chiefs’ away game against the Denver Broncos today, the singer wasn’t seen at Empower Field.

Swift was out earlier in the day in New York City, where she participated in a Q&A during a Toy Story 5 screening. She wrote the film’s song “I Knew It, I Knew You,” which has Oscar buzz.

Swift hasn’t been spotted at any of Kelce’s away games for the past two seasons, with security risks reportedly factoring into the decision. The tight end has a home game next Sunday, though, so she could be out in full force to support him and his teammates then as they face off against the New York Jets at Arrowhead Stadium.

While she has regularly attended Kelce’s games throughout their three-year relationship, Swift has never deliberately drawn attention to her appearances. As she told Time in December 2023, “I’m just there to support Travis. I have no awareness of if I’m being shown too much and pissing off a few dads, Brads, and Chads.”

“I don’t know how they know what suite I’m in,” she added. “There’s a camera, like, a half-mile away, and you don’t know where it is, and you have no idea when the camera is putting you in the broadcast, so I don’t know if I’m being shown 17 times or once.”

Last year, Swift attended some of Kelce’s games without being photographed. Over the course of her career, she has mastered the art of entering and leaving places undetected. In an October 2025 interview with Scott Mills on BBC Radio 2, she revealed that this would be her greatest asset as a spy.

“Just put me in a garbage can, roll me, I don’t care,” she said. “Honestly, I can fit in like, a purse. Sometimes I just can’t deal with it [the paparazzi and attention] and in those times, I won’t deal with it. I’m just digging tunnels under every building I go into, airlifting in through the skylight.”

#6913. The Top Five Brands to Know This Winter

With sharp tailoring and standout knitwear, MR PORTER offers a variety of collections for dressing well in the cold.

Winter cold gives you a reason to wear the clothes that make getting dressed interesting: a proper wool coat, a substantial sweater, trousers with some weight to them, and boots built for more than walking from the car to the restaurant. Then the holiday parties start, and your usual uniform needs an upgrade. A good wardrobe should handle all of it without requiring you to develop a different personality for every occasion. And because you’re wearing more of your clothes at once, the details matter. Fit, fabric, and proportion can make the difference between looking put together and looking like you got dressed in the dark.

MR PORTER is a great place to start building that versatile wardrobe. Its assortment brings together brands with a clear understanding of what each does well, from Tom Ford’s sharp tailoring to Brunello Cucinelli’s easy sophistication and Begg & Co’s expertise in knitwear and accessories. Yoke offers a more experimental take on everyday dressing, while Mr P focuses on the everyday staples that make getting dressed easier. The common threads are thoughtful design, good materials, and construction that holds up to repeated wear.

We’ve picked two pieces from each brand that make a compelling case for adding them to your cold-weather wardrobe.

Tom Ford launched his namesake label in 2005 after revitalizing Gucci throughout the 1990s with a sexier, more provocative take on luxury. The brand is known for sharp tailoring, sleek silhouettes, and a glamorous sensibility that extends from eveningwear to leather goods and footwear. Today, designer Haider Ackermann leads its creative direction, carrying that legacy forward with his own modern vision.

The designer’s tailoring has always favored a sharp silhouette, and these brown wool tuxedo pants put a more relaxed spin on formalwear. Double pleats give the straight legs room to move, while pressed creases keep the shape crisp. Wear them with a black dinner jacket for a holiday party that calls for something beyond the usual uniform, or pair them with a fine-gauge black sweater and polished loafers for a winter dinner.

A good winter night-out look starts from the ground up. Tom Ford’s Chelsea boots bring a little rock-and-roll swagger to the occasion, with glossy, lizard-effect leather, sharply pointed toes, and stacked heels that give the classic slip-on silhouette a Western edge. They’re the sort of boots that can turn a dark suit into a statement or give black jeans and a wool coat some much-needed attitude. Wear them to a holiday party, a late dinner, or anywhere your sneakers might send the wrong message.

Founded in Umbria in 1978, Brunello Cucinelli began with a simple idea: make cashmere sweaters for women in colors that felt fresh and modern. The Italian label has since expanded into a full menswear and womenswear collection, becoming known for luxurious knitwear, fine tailoring, and an easy approach to dressing well. Its clothes combine traditional Italian craftsmanship with a relaxed sophistication that works just as well for everyday wear as it does for more formal occasions.

Brunello Cucinelli has built a following around luxurious fabrics and Italian tailoring that manage to feel relaxed. This wool-felt overshirt puts that approach to work in a more casual silhouette, with flap chest pockets, silver-tone snap fastenings, and a curved hem. The black wool is substantial enough to take the edge off a cold city morning, while the clean design makes it a smart alternative to a traditional jacket. Wear it over a fine-knit sweater with wool trousers for the office, then head straight to dinner.

Brunello Cucinelli’s cashmere expertise is on full display in this black ribbed zip-up sweater, knitted in Italy with a relaxed fit and a stand collar that keeps the cold off your neck. The two-way zipper lets you adjust the coverage, whether you’re layering it under a wool overcoat for a weekend spent exploring the city or wearing it on its own during a long flight. It’s a clever way to make travel-day comfort feel considerably more considered.

Mr P. has an advantage most menswear labels don’t: it was developed by the people at MR PORTER who spend their days studying what makes clothes worth buying. Launched in 2017, the in-house brand focuses on wardrobe staples, from knitwear and denim to outerwear, with an emphasis on quality materials and details that make sense in everyday life. The idea is to get the fundamentals right, then give men plenty of ways to wear them.

A polo shirt can do more than a button-down when the occasion calls for looking presentable without getting too dressed up. Mr P.’s long-sleeved version is knitted in Italy from 75 percent merino wool and 25 percent silk, giving the fine-gauge fabric a soft feel and subtle sheen. An open collar and ribbed trims keep the design streamlined. A smart alternative to another crewneck sweater, this can be worn with flannel trousers for a holiday lunch or under a tailored coat when you’re meeting friends for a winter matinee.

Some winter days require a coat that does more than keep you warm. Mr P.’s green shearling number has plenty of personality, with a suede exterior, plush lining, and a broad collar that frames the face. The loose fit leaves room for a chunky sweater underneath, while the button front and slanted pockets keep things practical. Wear it with dark denim and sturdy boots for a Saturday spent browsing record shops, grabbing lunch, or walking home the long way. You might even forget how cold it is.

Founded by designer Norio Terada in Tokyo in 2018, Yoke takes its name from the garment panel that joins pieces of fabric together. That idea of connection carries through the label’s approach to design, which draws on art and reworks familiar menswear with unexpected proportions, interesting textures, and subtle twists. The result is clothing that feels relaxed and easy to wear, with enough edge to keep a wardrobe of everyday staples from looking predictable.

Yoke approaches everyday clothing with a Japanese design sensibility that puts proportion and considered details at the forefront. These wide-leg jeans show how much a change in silhouette can do. Cut from black cotton denim, they have a roomy leg and a classic five-pocket design, with enough volume to balance a chunky knit or a boxy winter coat. Wear them with a pair of leather sneakers for an afternoon antique hunting or checking out a gallery.

Made in Japan from a blend of 85 percent wool and 15 percent cashmere, this gray felt overcoat has a modern silhouette withdropped shoulders, wide notch lapels, and an oversized double-breasted cut that leaves plenty of room for winter layers. The fabric feels substantial without weighing you down, making it the perfect choice for days when you’re walking across town rather than hopping between cabs. Throw it over a chunky sweater and wide-leg jeans for a look that feels polished without being overly formal.

Begg & Co’s story begins in Paisley, Scotland, in 1866, when Alex Begg established a textile business that would become known for its scarves and cashmere. Today, the brand makes knitwear and accessories at mills in Ayr and Hawick, combining traditional Scottish textile techniques with modern design. Its cashmere collection ranges from classic scarves to substantial sweaters, with texture and color often doing the work that logos do elsewhere.

Begg & Co has been making textiles in Scotland since the 19th century, and its experience shows in the details of this navy scarf. The Bernard is knitted from a blend of 56 percent cashmere and 44 percent lambswool, with a repeating cable pattern that gives it texture and wide ribbed ends that keep the design clean. At nearly 7 feet long, it can be wrapped snugly around your neck on a windy morning or left loose over a wool coat when you want a little more warmth on the way to a weekend lunch.

Begg & Co’s Surfer cardigan is knitted in Scotland from pure cashmere, with blended yarns that give the ribbed surface subtle tonal variation. Horn buttons, welt pockets, and a shawl collar lend it a classic look, while the substantial knit makes it a practical alternative to a lightweight jacket. Wear it over a T-shirt with relaxed trousers for a slow Sunday morning at home, then keep it on when you head out for coffee or an impromptu lunch. It’s an easy way to look put together without getting dressed up.

#6909. Zoë Kravitz and Harry Styles Had Very Different Costumes for His Harryween Concerts

Harry Styles celebrated his Harryween shows this weekend, and he and his fiancée, Zoë Kravitz, got into the spirit.

On October 30, the singer performed the first of his two Halloween-themed concerts at New York City’s Madison Square Garden, where he wrapped up his 30-show residency on Saturday. The singer used his annual Harryween concert as an opportunity to pay homage to Elvis Presley and a past version of himself.

For his Presley-inspired look, Styles took the stage wearing a white bedazzled jumpsuit with colorful embellishments that featured a cobalt blue cape attachment and gold-trimmed sunglasses.

Kravitz watched from the audience and embraced a more traditional look in a red jacket and matching skirt, tights, and devil horns. She was joined by actress Riley Keough, who is Presley’s granddaughter. Keough was spotted filming the performance and dancing along to 2019’s “Watermelon Sugar.”

In addition to paying tribute to Presley’s stage wardrobe, Styles recreated his 2010 X Factor audition look, honoring the moment that launched his career. He was just 16 when he was on the show.

For his second Harryween show on October 31, Styles and Kravitz decided to pay homage to New York City. Styles wore a giant, fuzzy rat costume, complete with a giant foam slice of pizza, a nod to the city’s viral pizza rat.

Kravitz, meanwhile, dressed up as a takeout bag. The actress wore a white mini bubble dress with the instantly recognizable smiley and slogan printed on it. She finished the look with black kitten heels.

Styles first introduced Harryween in 2021 during his Love on Tour run, when he dressed as Dorothy from The Wizard of Oz for the first night and a Pierrot clown for the second. The following year, he channeled Danny Zuko from Grease for a Halloween performance in Los Angeles.

#6893. Designers Say These Are the 10 Best Antiques to Find at Estate Sales

Thrift stores and flea markets are popular spots for finding unusual vintage pieces, but they’re not the only options. Estate sales let you browse antiques in the very homes where they’ve been used and collected over the years. And since many items haven’t passed through a dealer, they may cost less than similar finds at an antique shop.

That combination makes estate sales a favorite among interior designers. They’re a source of characterful pieces for both client projects and personal collections. Krystal Reinhard, founder and principal designer of Old Soul Design Studio, likes being able to see items in the settings where they’ve long belonged. “It makes it easier to spot quality, scale, and patina,” she says.

For Valerie Helgeson, founder and lead designer at Design Directions, the appeal of estate sales is the fast-paced nature of the hunt. “There’s an immediacy to them that I like. You have to make a decision and trust your instincts,” she shares.

Whether you’re after a bargain, an authentic antique, or the fun of finding an unexpected treasure, estate sales are worth exploring. Ahead, interior designers reveal the 10 antiques they always look for—and explain why each belongs on your estate sale wish list.

Paige Loperfido, founder and principal designer of Decor & More Design Studio, is always game to grab hand-painted porcelain, midcentury ceramics, and antique glassware, like ice buckets and goblets.

“These collectibles bring color, pattern, and personality to kitchens and dining rooms,” she says. “Beyond their practical use, they easily transition into art, with vintage plates mounted on walls for a dynamic display, or grouped on open shelves. In dining spaces, glassware and tableware act as statement pieces, highlighting one-of-a-kind treasures that feel timeless, sophisticated, and thoughtfully curated, adding both charm and character to special gatherings.”

Reinhard calls antique candlestick holders “an instant layer,” with their warmth, height, and “a little romance with a surprisingly big impact for a small spend.” She adds that she often styles them as matched pairs on mantels and consoles, or as loose trios at varying heights on dining tables and coffee tables.

Going into estate sales with an open mind, Helgeson typically finds herself buying something she “could have never imagined looking for.” But frequently, one item tops her wish list, and that’s copper kitchenware. She displays these antique pieces in kitchens alongside other estate sale–found items, such as turned-wood bowls, old cutting boards, and bread bowls.

Pottery is a designer darling, since these pieces add texture and even imperfection (in a good way) to spaces, as Reinhard puts it. She tracks down pottery at estate sales, then showcases it on open shelving, sideboards, and tablescapes. She prefers to pair pottery with “something refined,” like brass, stone, or glass, to keep the mix elevated.

Loperfido’s favorite estate sale gems are what she calls “culturally rich pieces” that have been collected by homeowners from around the world. Think: Chinese antiques like foo dogs and ginger jars, and vintage ceramics such as majolica fish candlesticks, figurative pieces, or midcentury glazed pottery.

Oftentimes, she says that these particular pieces “showcase incredible craftsmanship and character,” adding that they’re perfect to layer on surfaces throughout the house, from bookshelves to nightstands to end tables.

At estate sales, Helgeson is scanning the spaces for “furniture with a strong silhouette.” Acting as anchors in rooms, Helgeson believes in accessorizing the rest of these spaces with vintage finds, which she says makes even new pieces appear like collected treasures.

“Vintage rugs bring color, pattern, and age instantly,” Reinhard remarks. She decorates with them “everywhere”: in powder rooms, mudrooms, and under seating areas to anchor the furniture and add depth.

“Original art can feel inaccessible at gallery pricing, but estate sales are one of the best places to find something truly special for a fraction of the cost,” Reinhard points out. She goes for single statement pieces, small collected clusters, or creates a full gallery wall that combines vintage art with modern pieces and personal photos.

Continually on the lookout for leather-bound collections and cocktail books, Reinhard snaps up antique books at estate sales.

“They’re one of the easiest ways to add personality and story—especially art, history, and travel titles that feel timeless and intentional,” she says. “I use them everywhere—stacked on coffee tables, tucked onto consoles, layered into shelves, or used as risers for objects to create height and visual rhythm.”

Loperfido says that these “finely detailed etched artworks” are often overlooked at estate sales, but they can instantly add sophistication and historical texture to any space.

“Small but rich in visual storytelling, they work beautifully against neutral or patterned backgrounds,” she notes.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6889. These 7 Storage Solutions for Small Spaces Look Too Good to Hide

Display-worthy baskets, glass-fronted cabinets, and colorful tins can make organization part of your decor.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

From a Brooklyn studio to a narrow Baltimore rowhouse, two decades of small-space living have taught me how to make every square foot count. Add in my Type A Virgo tendencies and an appreciation for good design, and I have strong opinions about storage: Namely, it should be as good-looking as it is hard-working. My first stop when hunting for products that deliver just that is always IKEA. The Swedish retailer has an unmatched selection of storage furniture and decor. Here are seven pieces that offer both function and form.

I have never known the pleasure of a linen closet, but I’ve gotten so many compliments on this metal-and-glass cabinet in my bathroom that I no longer mind. The reeded glass offers an unexpected texture while obscuring what’s inside to reduce visual clutter, and each interior shelf is roomy enough to hold a stack of towels, plus a small bin for toiletries and medicine bottles. I like to use the bamboo UPPDATERA boxes for material contrast; the larger style works great on the lower shelves to tame my mess of serums, makeup, lotions, and the like.

There is no such thing as too many bins or baskets when you live in a small space. They are the unsung organizational heroes, giving you a way to stash all the miscellaneous things that would typically be tucked away in a closet, attic, basement, or even a spare room in a larger home. IKEA sells a huge variety, but the woven styles like this one are particularly lovely to keep on display. I use them on my EKET living room shelves for games, in my bedroom for scarves and bags, and in the kitchen for tea towels and napkins.

When square footage is at a premium, closets tend to get eliminated. A free-standing wardrobe is the solution to creating clothes storage in a small bedroom. This chic new design features sliding doors—a space-saver itself since you won’t need to account for door clearance—adjustable shelves, and two drawers.

If your front door, like mine, opens directly into your living space, you need to fake a foyer as best you can. The key to that is shoe storage—otherwise, guests will be staring at muddy boots the minute they walk into your home. This cabinet hides 14 pairs behind a traditional design, and sticks out from the wall less than a foot. Two drawers make space for keys and sunglasses while four hooks give bags and dog leashes a home.

All kitchens can benefit from more storage, but it’s the small cook spaces where you really need to get creative. A mobile cart offers that utility, and this one features three shelves for anything from spices and baking supplies to serveware or a coffee bar.

Another simple small kitchen storage idea: Keep your dry goods in stackable containers, like the BETTHAJ tins, to maximize your pantry shelf space. They’re far easier to handle than flimsy paper containers or plastic bags, plus, they look great on a countertop or open shelving. They come in five different sizes, from 19 oz. to 3.3 qt., so you can mix and match to suit your needs.

Why choose between extra seating or more storage when you can have both? This sturdy little pouf hides a compartment that I use for an extra throw blanket, but it could also fit a few games, your knitting supplies, or remote controls. The cushioned top is taut enough that it can also function as a side table, making this a small space triple threat.

Head to IKEA to find storage that maximizes space without sacrificing style.

#6887. The 20 Best- and Worst-Dressed Celebs at the 2026 CFDA Awards

Tom + Lorenzo recap the 45th annual CFDAs red carpet as only they can.

Everyone always says that the Met Gala is fashion’s biggest night, but for our money, if you really want to see celebrities trying to outdo each other and impress the world of fashion with their style, the CFDA Awards is where it’s at. The Met Gala is a fashion parade, but the CFDAs are more of a fashion battle. After all, wouldn’t you want to impress the Council of Fashion Designers of America? Let’s see who won the fashion war this year and who had to retreat and regroup for next time. Here are our picks for the best and worst of the night.

While we think a floor-length leather coat can be dramatic on a red carpet, it’s really all about what you pair it with, and we just don’t think this somewhat drab dusty pink dress does much for the look. It gives the impression she threw a coat on over a nightgown because her hotel room was on fire in the middle of the night. A coat like that needs a stronger design underneath it. Cute bag, but it doesn’t vibe well with the rest of it.

We know we should probably tut-tut her antics, but we honestly think she’s one of the most interesting people you could ever hope to invite to a red carpet event. She always delivers a look. It may not be a chic look or a high-end look or even a pretty look, but it’s always bold and leaves heads turning. What else is a red carpet for? The design gets a little sloppy at the hem, but it’s a witty and well-styled look. We just don’t love the matchy slides. The tiara is hilarious.

It’s very old-school debutante and she’s well-suited to it, but it sits too low on her. We certainly don’t have a problem with chic, elegant restraint in style, but we do think you need to do something with it to make it red carpet-ready. There’s nothing wrong with plainly pretty, but a metallic shoe, a pendant, some hair with a little life to it—all of these would improve the look, we think.

It’s very showgirl, but that’s what you get when you pick a vintage Bob Mackie. We don’t know if any of his most celebrated divas wore this, but it’s definitely giving Cher, Ann-Margret, and Tina Turner, which can never be bad references for any look. Our only quibble is with the center slit and the hem length, but we love the gold sandals. She’s giving throwback glam and she looks great.

We half-love this. The hair is exquisite, the jacket is beautifully tailored, and we love the delicate jewelry and lace touches. But we’re sorry to say, everything below the waist is disappointing. If anyone could make a pair of bloomers look chic, it’s probably them, but if you’re not going to commit to wearing tights with them, we don’t see the point. The pants bring the whole look down.

This is lovely. Polka dots are pretty trendy at the moment, and there were several takes on this red carpet, but this was our favorite. It’s a little rare to see the print rendered in anything but black and white, so this pale and pretty blue was a pleasant surprise. We’re not the biggest fans of floppy bows, but we like that this one provides a sharp contrast with the rest of the dress. The ruching is lightly applied and the styling is flawless.

We’ve been banging the “sheer dresses are played out” drum for at least a year now, but designers, stylists, and stars love them way too much. Our one rule about the style is to make it interesting, because there’s nothing worse than showing off your underwear in a boring dress. This is anything but boring. It’s borderline drag queen, but that’s not a bad thing. The feather effect on the cuffs, collar, and hem are fun and fabulous, but it’s her hair and makeup that really sell this look.

Now that’s what we’re talking about! Why come to the CFDAs to be normal? This is an insane design, but what impresses us most about this look is how well-styled she is. Her hair and makeup are very pretty, and her jewelry choices are fairly low-key. You really don’t want your styling to be fighting the dress, which doesn’t need any help. We can’t imagine she had an easy time sitting all night, but we’re not opposed to a little suffering in order to serve a look.

If there’s one style of dress we dislike above all others, it’s the “my dress is falling off” dress. We tend to think it’s gimmicky, and we also tend to think it’s weird to pretend you’re getting undressed in public. Having said that, we’re surprised to admit how much we love this. The color story is elegant, the exposed bustier is very pretty, and we love how the blocking gives a very simple dress so much visual interest. The relatively clean and low-key styling is perfect.

We’re sorry, but we simply just don’t like long, sheer overlays over miniskirts. The colors look amazing on her, but we’re not sure we’ve ever seen a dress that gave such a strong impression that it isn’t finished yet. This is like one-third of a good design, and it should’ve been left on the dress form until it was finished.

Designer Zac Posen has been doing a bang-up job of changing the image of Gap by putting high-end custom pieces on the red carpet that work with Gap styles and textiles. What could be more Gap than a red t-shirt and classic denim jacket? We like how the tee has been altered, and the jewelry touches really help elevate the look. It’s cute, it’s chic, and it feels like it came in off the street—in a good way.

Go on, girl. Give us nothing. We’re surprised she picked something so sedate and relatively casual to wear, since she’s usually pretty good about serving a look. Nothing about this says “gala” or even “night.” It feels like she stopped by on her way from work. The skirt’s okay, but the top is a big nothing.

It was a big night for crop tops and cutouts, and we wish we could say that we love this take, but it’s a tad over-designed. We tend to hate tassels on sight, and we can’t say this look is changing our mind. The color looks spectacular and we like the design of the bust and halter, but the fit is too tight. All of the embellishments are overdone and could have used an edit. Those hip beads look like they’d be pretty annoying all night.

This is kind of an oddly unbalanced look. The textile is beautiful, but the design leaves it looking a little flimsy. At the very least, a slightly more interesting bandeau would have helped the look; maybe with some pleating to give it shape. We also think you need to really dress up a crop top for the red carpet so it doesn’t look too “poolside.” She needs some statement jewelry. We don’t understand the black train or sandals at all.

To us, this is an instance of a beautiful textile being used badly. It’s a rich, luxurious-looking metallic brocade that would make a fantastic coat or ball gown, but it’s too stiff for a crop top and knee-length skirt. We also don’t think it photographs all that well and the color does nothing for her.

Elegant, but a tad boring. Plain black gowns need smart styling to help them stand out, and while we get that she’s not really into a lot of flash in her style, this really could’ve used better jewelry and a more interesting wrap.

This one took us a minute. At first glance, we disliked the color and we felt the design was a little stale, but the longer we took it in, the more it started to appeal to us. The design is deceptive, because it comes across like a standard goddess gown, but the pleating and draping give it real interest and shape. We love the bold lip, which really helps set that pale green off. We might have liked a little bit more in the jewelry department, but the delicate pieces are lovely.

Can a girl ever really go wrong in a hot metallic dress and a killer pair of earrings? This is a nice change of pace for her, since she tends to go for slip dresses and sheer dresses more often than not. We love how the bodice looks like they just brushed gilt onto her. The crocodile-like texture gives it more of an old world armored feel, but the shape is pure modern sexy. Those slingbacks are gorgeous.

It’s an impressive design, and it fits with Zac Posen’s plans to glam up the brand, but turning denim into a formal gown is always a little tricky. We tend to think it works best if you don’t overwork the textile and if you don’t make too many cutesy references to jean pockets or buttons. In other words, a dress that looks like it was made out of old jeans just doesn’t look chic to us. The bodice works, but the skirt is not great.

This is a lot of fun and she looks great in it. We keep coming back to the same point about crop tops and bra tops on a formal red carpet: They need some oomph in the styling. To be fair, this design is bold and bright and doesn’t need too much help, but the color blocking gives it a sporty feel, and we can’t help thinking some bright bangles, a cocktail ring, and a more interesting shoe choice would really elevate this.

#6885. The Author Behind the Pseudonym Rebecca Morrow Was a Total Mystery—Until Now

Rainbow Rowell is the bestselling writer behind the novel Corinne. Here, she explains why it was finally time to come forward.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

In 2022, the novel Corinne was published. At the story’s center is Corinne, a woman who walked away from family and her fundamentalist upbringing but finds herself unable to forget Enoch, her first love. Rebecca Morrow, the name on the book’s cover, was described as a “pseudonym for a New York Times bestselling author,” and though readers guessed at who that author might be, Rebecca Morrow’s identity remained a mystery until now.

Rebecca Morrow is Rainbow Rowell, the celebrated author of Cherry Baby and Eleanor & Park, among others. Corinne isn’t about her life, but it has parallels, and in 2022, publishing something so personal felt impossible. Now denying what she has come to consider her favorite book seems unthinkable. In July 2027, Corinne will be reissued with Rowell’s name on the cover, and in 2028 she will publish Catch Us the Foxes, a Corinne sequel.

Here Rowell talks about the decision to write Corinne—and deciding to finally put her name on it—in her own words.

In the darkest hours of the COVID-19 pandemic—when the future seemed uncertain, and the rest of the world felt far away—I did something very strange for me.

I wrote a book, knowing that I couldn’t let anyone read it.

“I just need to do this,” I told my literary agent. “And then I’ll write something we can sell.” It was a good time to sell. I’d just published three No. 1 bestsellers in a row. “I just need to get this out of me first.”

I wrote the first draft of the novel almost feverishly. It was about a woman who is cast out of her strict Christian church as a teenager and cut off by her family. She comes back home as an adult to reckon with the past—and with the man who was the catalyst for her exile.

Really, the book was about all the things I’d always been too scared to write about, or even to talk about….Faith. Shame. Sex. (More sex. Unclean sex. Forbidden sex. Sex in the same room as a Bible.) And, most thornily, how it feels to leave a fundamentalist church. The way you eventually realize that you can’t leave. That you’ll always take it with you, because you were made there. Baked there. Poured and set.

When I was done with the manuscript, it was my favorite thing I’d ever written. Maybe not my best book, but already the one I loved the most.

“But honestly,” I told my agent, “we can’t do anything with this. It’s too personal. And it’s the very last thing any of my readers would want.” I was writing queer YA fantasy. Not whatever this was.

My agent, being an agent, asked if he could read it anyway.

And he liked it. (He liked my beautiful baby!)

We very quickly began talking about a way to publish the book that might sidestep all my fears and anxieties….

There began my strange—and still hardly believable to me—journey as Rebecca Morrow, the pseudonymous author of Corinne.

It was exciting at first. It was different and fun. A pseudonym! A secret identity! A way to drop a novel into the world without having to talk about it, or sell it. Without having to worry about alienating or recruiting my existing readers.

The pseudonym satisfied that still feverish part of me that needed to let the story out, but was too terrified to stand by it.

Oh, I knew it was absurd to write a book about shame and secrets, and then to keep it a secret. (Don’t think this didn’t come up in therapy!) But at the time, it seemed like the only path forward for Corinne. It was this or let the characters die on my hard drive.

My editor and I agonized over how to handle the pseudonym. Traditionally, authors write fake biographies to go with their fake names. But I didn’t want to get caught in a lie if I was found out. And I didn’t want to seek out undeserved goodwill as a “debut author.”

The marketing department really wanted something they could say about Rebecca Morrow. They suggested “lives in Nebraska,” but even that felt like divulging too much. We settled on “Rebecca Morrow is a pseudonym for a New York Times bestselling author.”

My apologies to the bestselling, usually Mormon, authors who got blamed for this weird book. I didn’t see that coming.

I did expect readers to guess that I was the author. Even though I’d freed myself to write Corinne in a new voice and shaken off some of my more recognizable habits, the book ended up sounding more like me than ever. It was like my voice distilled.

Some people did guess. It didn’t bother me. I still didn’t have to talk about the book. I didn’t have to face it.

Corinne is my favorite of my books and beyond that, Enoch and Corinne, the man and woman at its center, are my all-time favorite characters. It’s hard to explain why…

I always write messy, flawed characters, but these two are my messiest and most flawed. Their love is not the stuff of romance. (This novel is not a romance; it will disappoint you if you come to it looking for reassurance or escape.)

Neither Corinne nor Enoch is based on me, though they carry many of my burdens. This is not a hidden memoir. But they feel so real to me. They make mistakes, and I want to forgive them. I want them to find grace. I think about them still, all the time. I worry about what’s become of them.

Ultimately, that’s why I’m stepping forward to claim the book now. At some point, “not having to talk about Corinne” turned into “not being able to talk about Corinne,” even when I desperately wanted to.

I love this book! I want to put it on my shelf, between Any Way the Wind Blows and Scattered Showers. I want readers who liked Slow Dance and Cherry Baby to discover it.

I loved the characters in Corinne so much that I wrote a sequel about them—Catch Us the Foxes—and I want that book to have a life, too. William Morrow is launching both books, properly—Corinne next July, and Catch Us the Foxes in March 2028—in beautiful hardcover editions.

I revised Corinne for the new edition—I went a little deeper on the church stuff this time, and sharpened the focus on some of the characters. It’s a little longer. And, I think, a little better. My favorite designer, Jim Tierney, designed the new matching covers.

I still get a surge of fear and excitement every time I see my name there front and center.

My therapist says that I’m very good at compartmentalizing—keeping some parts of myself shut away from other parts. I would argue that this is a very useful skill! But it does bad things to your soul, over time...being fractured like that.

But as the years have passed, it’s felt like I’ve left something half done. Like Corinne is a truth half shared. A story half told. I needed to get the book out of me, but I ended up cutting myself off from it. That was never the goal.

I’m grateful to “Rebecca Morrow” for making Corinne possible, but I’m finally ready to bring these characters home.

#6883. Sarah J. Maas Has a Taylor Swift Problem

Both women are at the height of their game, but building an empire comes with a price.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Welcome back to Prythian, the fictional faerie world Sarah J. Maas has held us hostage in for years now (complimentary). A Court of Splintered Harmony, the sixth book in Sarah’s hugely successful A Court of Thorns and Roses (ACOTAR) series, hit shelves on October 27, 2026, after a five-year wait from its predecessor. That sound you’re hearing right now? That’s the internet collectively falling into Maas Hysteria.

There’s no denying the chokehold Sarah has over romantasy readers. Ever since she exploded onto BookTok during the pandemic, her career has taken on a pop-star level of celebrity status. She sells out bookstores for midnight book release parties like Harry Styles sold out his MSG residency. Professional celeb gossiper Deuxmoi covers blind items about her. To announce the next two books in her ACOTAR series, she went on Alex Cooper’s Call Her Daddy podcast. You know, the same venue Chappell Roan used to reveal her new relationship status and Zayn Malik used to break his six-year interview hiatus.

It’s fitting that her career has taken off like a pop star’s because each new release feels like a Taylor Swift album drop. The noise on social media surrounding their new releases could literally shake the ground you walk on—and you just know they’re going to immediately sell out everywhere. Both their audiences are hugely engaged with sky-high, emotionally specific expectations, but it’s also what’s starting to get them into trouble.

Both women have reached godlike levels of commercial success in their respective fields—and each has faced questions about whether the artistry of their recent work still matches that commercial success. Like Taylor, Sarah’s success and her brand might be the very things usurping her creative output.

Taylor and Sarah built their careers on being true Machiavellians. They have visions for their work that extend beyond one album or book. Sarah described her latest installments to the ACOTAR world as “a four-part volume-defying narrative.” Part one takes place in the newly released A Court of Splintered Harmony (ACOTAR 6). Parts two and three will be released together in January as A Court of Forgotten Melody (ACOTAR 7). Part four has yet to be announced.

If that sounds confusing, well, welcome to the Maasverse. Like Taylor, Sarah has created a universe for her fans complete with its own languages, inside jokes, and, of course, an intricate network of fan theories that would take an FBI task force decades to disentangle. Not only does each book in her series build on the last, exposing twists and turns that were hinted at four or five books ago, but she leaves breadcrumbs for readers from the larger Sarah J. Maas multiverse connecting her three worlds together: Erilia (Throne of Glass), Prythian (ACOTAR), and Midgard (Crescent City).

And it’s making her millions. Since 2015, Maas has sold more than 75 million copies. Her net worth sits somewhere around $60 million. She’s also building on it in a way that was previously only reserved for the likes of The Hunger Games and Twilight—books that crossed over to Hollywood via box-office-breaking films—even with a canceled TV project that is back on the market after several years of development hell. Target released its own ACOTAR-themed collection. Knix dropped a line of lingerie, complete with court-specific panties and negligees (the Nesta Silver Flames corset retails for $150). The series has even ventured into the world of luxury goods with its For Love & Lemons collection, where a Night Court minidress will run you almost $700.

In terms of critical success, though, both women have seen a downward trend with their last works: Taylor’s The Life of a Showgirl and Sarah’s Crescent City: House of Flame and Shadow.

Kirkus called House of Flame and Shadow “pure entertainment, for better or for worse.” Meanwhile, Miss Willa’s Book Club, a popular account on TikTok, said it was an “info dump” noting that, “It was so long and so dense that I absorbed so much less of it.”

“This is kind of the issue with these authors who decide to build these huge universes,” BookTokker Antoinette Cea-Lopes says. “The audiences are left waiting until the end of the universe in order to determine whether or not the author is any good.”

It speaks to the pressure monoliths like Sarah and Taylor face when releasing new work. There is an intense pressure to write more and release faster than ever. However, it’s not enough to write a good story anymore; it needs to be epic. It needs to bring in new fans while upping the stakes for old fans.

When fan expectations are this high, how can anyone—even the reigning queen of faerie smut—ever live up to that hype? Where is the line between giving fans more at the expense of good writing?

As a longtime Maassassin myself (yes, she totally has her own form of Swifties), I hold the bar high because I know what she is capable of. I’ll admit that when she announced a four-part volume-defying narrative, I worried the art was being sacrificed in the name of having a Maasverse. The bigger the worlds got and the more they crossed over, the less attention to detail there would be in the writing. As much as I’d like to see Manon and Nesta have a queen off in the future, that doesn’t mean it serves the larger narrative arc. Precision has always been Sarah’s strength, but the ballooning of her worldview feels more like a brand move and not an artistic one.

Taylor, too, has been accused of putting the brand before the art. The Life of a Showgirl has fans split on whether the album represents a deeply personal account of her happily ever after era or if it’s a full-on marketing cash grab.

The truth is good art is counterintuitive to good branding. It requires time and space, something Sarah, who publishes at a breakneck pace these days (16 books in 14 years over multiple series, to be exact), hasn’t taken advantage of since the early days of her career.

But that’s the thing about branding—it requires a near-constant stream of content. When Sarah first started gaining popularity on TikTok, she already had two completed series for readers to dive into: Throne of Glass and ACOTAR. She then decided to revisit the ACOTAR world and turn it from a trilogy into a massive multi-part series.

Even if I don’t always know where Sarah is going with a plotline, there’s a moment at the end of each book when, as she would say, even the gods give pause. A moment when I can feel in my bones something big is on the horizon. When the story shifts from character study to fantasy epic. But those high-impact moments were missing from the last two ACOTAR offerings, A Court of Frost and Starlight and A Court of Silver Flames.

Despite the criticism, there’s a reason Taylor and Sarah have amassed the fan bases they have and that stick with them no matter what products they produce. Even my recent disappointment won’t stop me from purchasing future reads by her (shoutout to Trope Bookstore’s midnight release party!). Part of being a Maassassin—and a Swiftie—is a willingness to go on the journey with them. And as they’ve both proven, they operate well from the underdog position. Just when the world counts them out, that’s when they strike the hardest.

Although I sometimes like to think of myself as a Suriel (hot, pale, mysterious, gossipy), right now, I can’t see the truth when it comes to the future of Sarah J. Maas. I’ve been wrong about her before and I’m happy to be wrong again. Maybe she’s like the Asteri (IYKYK) gobbling down the book market and pop culture at large—at the risk of her own implosion. Or maybe she’s the true High Queen of Faerie, just biding her time until her vision reaches maturation.

One of her characters once asked, “What if we go on only to more pain and despair?” Dramatically, that is sometimes how I feel as a reader. But I’m reminded of Sarah’s aptly written response to the question, a response that reflects all my feelings about reader loyalty: “Then it is not the end.”

#6878. Why Tatyana Ali’s Subtle ‘DWTS’ Team Dance Comment Has Fans Fully Speculating

It has DWTS fans connecting some very dramatic dots.

The people’s princess of Dancing With the Stars, Tatyana Ali, isn’t holding back after last night’s team dance. Tatyana seemingly confirmed the second fan was on point with their theory when she responded, “Very much so.”

Either way…so happy to see Tatyana and Jan are still in the running to win the coveted Mirrorball Trophy!

#6875. 14 Best Eye Creams for Puffiness, According to Dermatologists and Editor Testing

Send your under-eye bags packing with these top-tested picks.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

No matter how many hours I sleep I always seem to wake up with puffy eyes—and there’s only so much under-eye concealer can wear to make my eyes look less tired. My solution? Incorporating a depuffing eye cream (and the occasional cold compress) into my skincare routine—specifically, one of the 15 eye creams for puffiness I've rounded up below. Trust me when I say our editors have tested hundreds of options, from drugstore eye creams to eye creams that smooth wrinkles, and these are absolute standouts.

I'm not the only one obsessed with these depuffing eye creams. I consulted three dermatologists—Joshua Zeichner, MD, Jessica Cheung, MD, and Adrienne O'Connell, DO—who shared their favorite eye creams for puffy eyes, along with their expert tips for choosing the right one for your skin types and concerns.

Whether you're looking for a lightweight formula that won't pill under makeup or a rich and creamy option for dry skin, I found depuffing eye creams for every skin type and concern.

Why we love it: Although this is billed as a lifting and firming eye cream, it’s fantastic for depuffing. The formula uses harungana extract, a natural retinol alternative, to help tighten skin, which can make fluid retention under your eyes appear less noticeable. Plus, there’s caffeine to help improve the microcirculation of your blood vessels, which can reduce the look of swelling and under-eye bags right away.

Cosmo editor’s review: "I applied product to my left eye only here to really see the difference. I make sure to go all around my eyes, including my full eyelid, crow's feet, and just below my brows. 30 seconds after application, my bags look significantly less pronounced, my brow is lifted, and my skin looks a little smoother."—BETH GILLETTE, BEAUTY EDITOR

Why we love it: Winner of the Cosmo Holy Grail Beauty Award for best depuffing eye cream, this gel formula uses a mix of lightweight hydrators, antioxidant-rich caffeine, and soothing ingredients to help calm down under-eye puffiness. The tip applicator, which stays chilled to even further reduce swelling, is an added touch.

Cosmo editor’s review: “This first entered my rotation when I was sleep-deprived in Korea. But now, I swipe it under my eyes nearly every day, regardless of how much sleep I’m working with, to help hydrate my delicate eye area and depuff any bags.” —BG

Why we love it: If you're willing to spend a little extra cash, we think Sunday Riley's Autocorrect eye cream is totally worth the $$$. Made with depuffing ingredients like caffeine and ginseng extract, this splurge-worthy solution is a favorite among Amazon shoppers and Cosmo editors alike.

"My skin is sooo picky when it comes to skincare—my bathroom shelf has basically become a graveyard for half-used eye creams that I’ve had to discard," says Samantha Jones, commerce editor. "But!! This (admittedly expensive) option from Sunday Riley is a staple in my routine now: I love the lightweight yet super-hydrating formula that absorbs easily, and the inclusion of caffeine makes a noticeable difference in reducing dark circles and puffiness."

Why we love it: Like a shot of espresso for your under-eyes, this caffeine-filled eye cream helps to temporarily constrict blood vessels, which boosts circulation and reduces puffiness. For a two-pronged approach to depuffing, I like to keep the tube in the fridge. The combo of caffeine and coldness are a match made in heaven for tackling puffiness and under-eye bags—especially on rough mornings.

"The caffeine is literally like a dose of awakening coffee for my eye area, and the cooling tip is great for de-puffing and massaging the product into my skin," Hyman says.

The fix? This top-rated retinol eye cream, which happens to be a favorite of mine and many dermatologists. “It contains a gentle form of retinol to strengthen under-eye skin and reduce the appearance of lines as well as puffiness and dark circles," Dr. Zeichner said.

Since its retinol-infused formula can make skin more sensitive to the sun, we recommend saving this cream for the evening as a part of your nighttime skincare routine.

Why we love it: One of my personal favorites on the list, this eye cream from celebrity makeup artist Charlotte Tilbury is nothing short of magical for your under-eyes. It has a very rich, almost balm-like consistency that traps moisture under the eyes so that the delicate (and often dry) skin in that area is left smooth and hydrated. Why we love it: CeraVe's Eye Repair Cream is an excellent choice for someone who is new to using an eye cream or anyone who has sensitive skin. Why we love it: The first time I tested this eye cream, I was hooked. I've been using it for as long as I can remember, and it consistently makes my eye area look more wide-awake. I wouldn’t say that it’s the number one eye cream for puffiness, but it does brighten like a champ, which helps draw attention away from any bags or puffiness under my eyes. It's also *perfect* for wearing beneath concealer and makeup thanks to a creamy, non-pilling formula and a banana-inspired tint that helps neutralize discoloration upon application.

It doesn’t depuff as noticeably as other products, but it plays well with others. That means I can layer it with a depuffer to erase dark circles and puffiness together.”—CM

One of the top ways to quickly get rid of puffiness is by gently pressing a cooling tool, or even a cold spoon, under your eyes. Lucky for you, this eye cream has a cooling ceramic tip applicator so you can leave your spoons in the kitchen. By massaging your under-eyes with the cooling tip as you apply the caffeine-and peptide-packed formula, you can double the depuffing benefits. This bb is an absolute staple in my morning skincare routine, especially after nights when I've had one-too-many margs at happy hour.

Glowing customer review: "This product is easy to apply and absorbs quickly. It makes concealer and foundation easier to put on. It blends well in the skin and the applicator is easy to use. A definite must-have in my daily beauty routine."

Why we love it: Dr. O’Connell says that this eye cream from Tru Alchemy is one of her favorites for depuffing and brightening the eye area. “The formula is a hybrid of a cream and a serum, meaning it penetrates the skin deeply like a serum and hydrates just like a cream," she said. It also has all of the key ingredients for depuffing, brightening, and smoothing. Think: hyaluronic acid, caffeine, vitamin C, niacinamide, and squalane.

Why we love it: No, these aren't technically an eye cream, but there aren’t many things that feel as good (or work as well) as popping on a set of under-eye patches after a late night. I slap on these caffeine-and hyaluronic acid-infused patches while I do my eye makeup to help hydrate and depuff my under-eyes and help catch any fallout from my eyeshadow. Plus, I love that they’re made of a bio-cellulose material that helps seal in moisture, but without feeling too sticky or slimy on my skin.

“When choosing an eye cream it is crucial to read the ingredient list,” Dr. O’Connell says. “There are a few key ingredients—including caffeine, peptides, and hyaluronic acid—that really work well at taking away puffiness, especially when combined, and so those are the ones you want to look for.”

Cheung also says that “ingredients that help firm and strengthen your skin, including retinol and antioxidants, will help to fight puffy eyes,” too.

Along with containing depuffing ingredients, many popular eye creams for puffy eyes come with a cooling, massage tip applicator (like Caudalie, Ilia, and Tru Alchemy, above) which helps with further depuffing. Plus, they feel incredible.

Everyone's skin is different, and an eye cream that works for one reviewer may not work for you. For example, if you have drier skin, you may prefer a richer formula like the Charlotte Tilbury Magic Eye Rescue Cream, while someone with oilier skin may have better results with a gel-based product like the Dieux Auracle Eye Gel.

Additionally, an eye cream that’s super potent and yields amazing results for one person may be too strong for someone with sensitive skin. We recommend patch-testing a product on your wrist to see if your skin is reactive to it before applying it to more sensitive areas like your eyes.

There are a few different methods for quickly reducing eye puffiness. To depuff your eyes fast, you can dunk your face in an ice bath or apply cold compresses to your eyes, Cheung says. She also says that placing chilled cucumber slices or damp tea bags on your eyes can depuff in a pinch as well.

Damp tea bags are especially useful because you'll get the cooling effects as well as some caffeine for reducing inflammation. Giving yourself a lymphatic massage (with your hands or an ice roller) can help to move any puffiness-inducing fluid around and deflate under-eye bags as well.

#6874. For His 40th Birthday, Drake Flexed a Custom Rolex and a $250,000 Watch in the Space of 24 Hours

The rapper marked his milestone birthday with two extravagant timepieces, including a one-of-a-kind Daytona worth millions.

Turning 40 is a milestone worth celebrating in style. And to nobody's surprise, Drake did just that.

As you'd expect, the Canadian rapper's birthday outfit was not exactly understated. In fact, his watch rotation featured not one, but two rather extraordinary Rolexes. Why settle for one when you can have two?

The first was a custom, sapphire-covered Daytona, while the second was a colorful Day-Date 36 with a reported six-figure price tag. There are certainly worse ways to welcome a new decade.

First up was the Rolex Cosmograph Daytona, a watch that makes even the most extravagant diamond-set timepieces look positively modest. The one-of-a-kind creation features a diamond-pavé dial, brilliant blue sapphire hour markers, and a bezel set with baguette-cut blue sapphires. But the real showstopper is the bracelet, which is covered in a gradient of blue sapphires that gradually fade into diamonds.

And if all those precious stones weren't enough to make your eyes water, the estimated price tag certainly might. The one-of-a-kind creation has reportedly been valued at anywhere between $2 million and $5 million. Although, given that Rolex made just one, it's difficult to put an exact figure on it.

While you might assume the rapper had commissioned the watch especially for his big birthday, he actually debuted it back in August. Still, when you've got something quite this spectacular sitting in your watch collection, it would be a shame not to give it another outing.

The second Rolex was a rather different kind of extravagance. Drake also showed off a rose-gold Day-Date 36 featuring the brand's famously playful puzzle dial. Instead of traditional hour markers, baguette-cut sapphires surround a dial made up of brightly colored jigsaw pieces. And rather than displaying the usual day and date, the apertures reveal inspirational words and emojis, including a little red heart. It's about as far from your grandfather's Rolex as you can get.

The watch has been valued at around $250,000 which, rather remarkably, makes it the considerably cheaper of Drake's two birthday watches.

Of course, Drake has never been one for doing things by halves, particularly when it comes to watches. And while most people would be more than happy to receive a single Rolex for their 40th, it seems the rapper couldn't quite settle on one.

#6873. What Kate Middleton Is Like Behind the Scenes, Revealed by New Royal Family Member Who Vacations With Her

The two recently spent time together in the Isles of Scilly.

The newest member of the royal family, Peter Phillips’s wife Harriet Sperling, has revealed what the Princess of Wales, Kate Middleton, is like behind the scenes, after vacationing with her, Prince William, and their three children.

Harriet opened up about their newly formed but seemingly already close bond in an interview with HELLO! magazine, during which she spoke about how her life has changed since marrying Princess Anne’s son. Explaining that she first met the Wales family while in the Isles of Scilly, Harriet said she has had “an incredible experience being welcomed in” by the senior royals.

Speaking about their shared holiday in particular, she added, “It was great because the children were all jumping into the water at the same time. It was very casual, but there is a clear level of respect there.” In addition to having a teenage daughter of her own (Georgina, born from her previous marriage to fitness instructor Antonio St. John Sperling), Harriet is now stepmother to Peter’s two children Savannah and Isla, whom he shares with his ex Autumn Kelly.

Harriet even went on to say that “I can look to Catherine for advice and guidance. She’s very open-hearted, and I would always feel comfortable asking her if I needed advice about something.”

Harriet and the Princess of Wales have plenty in common too, with both sharing a passion for raising awareness around infant mental health. Until recently, Harriet worked as an NHS nurse supporting children and their families, but after 24 years, she’s starting a new role focusing on early childhood development.

“Over time, I’ve become increasingly passionate about the importance of promoting healthy brain development,” she told HELLO! of the career change. “If we can support parents during this critical early period, we have an opportunity to make a lasting difference for children.”

According to the magazine, Harriet is now a patron for Home-Start Cotswolds, a charity offering “support, friendship, and practical help for families with young children.” The cause is reminiscent of Kate’s charity work with the Royal Foundation Centre for Early Childhood, such as her most recent project highlighting the importance of children spending time in nature.

#6867. Pamela Anderson’s Cranberry Nails Are the Most Elegant Fall Manicure: How to Recreate

Not only is the shape practical, but the color is timeless.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

I said it before, and I’ll say it again: Pamela Anderson has become one of the most unexpected beauty muses. From her pared-back approach to makeup to her ever-changing hairstyles, the actor has spent recent years setting trends that span generations. And her latest manicure is making a rather convincing case for swapping your usual autumnal burgundy for something a little juicier.

Taking to Instagram, Anderson recently shared a video reel documenting her recent evening spent gardening, reading, and simply living her best life. "Fall is the season of the soul… 🤍🍂," she wrote to caption the post. While the wholesome content had me questioning my own screen-time report (spoiler: it’s an unhealthy amount), it was her nails that left a lasting impression on the beauty editor in me. And she’s been rocking a versatile fall hue everywhere from her backyard to the CFDA Awards.

A far cry from her signature French manicure, Anderson debuted a glossy, cranberry-red shade painted onto short, squoval-shaped nails. Not only is the shape practical and universally flattering, but the color is timeless and an autumn manicure classic.

To recreate Pamela Anderson’s cranberry squoval nails, you’re going to want to invest some time into the prep. I’m talking trimming your cuticles, filing your nails down, and rounding the corners off for that signature square-meets-oval shape.

Then comes the polish. A bright-toned red like essie’s Forever Yummy or OPI’s Big Apple Red is going to be your best bet. Finish with a generous helping of a high-shine top coat for that freshly picked, almost wet-gloss look, and you’re good to go!

Who knew a trip down the fruit and vegetable aisle could double as a nail appointment? I’ll definitely be channeling the duality of Anderson both gardening and attending a chic red carpet with this fall-ready manicure.
