#6864. The Vintage Features That EVERY Homebuyer Wants Right Now, According to Experts

These are the authentic details that make a home extra special.

Pictured above: Designer Annie Kern showcased breathtaking stained glass windows throughout this historic home in Kansas City, Missouri.

I grew up in a family captivated—if not downright obsessed—by homes with character. My parents always took the long way home, winding slowly through our town's historic neighborhoods to admire the details on every house: immaculate stone walls, whimsical wrought-iron gates, ivy-covered façades, and shingles weathered to silvery perfection. Those drives became a kind of education, and each architectural flourish inspired the home I grew up in.

In recent decades, though, the real estate market has been flooded with sleek new builds and smart-home conveniences, which in turn have dimmed the prominence of intricate vintage details. Even worse, the structures often look incongruous with their surroundings. Prefab townhomes now rise from former parking lots, farmhouse-style mansions perch improbably on California sand, and shiny concrete slabs have replaced weathered shingles deep in the New England woods.

But, as with all trends, we’ve begun to circle back. Many experts are now seeing that buyers are rediscovering the charm of craftsmanship and are looking to prioritize authentic vintage features that just can't be faked. Ahead, we spoke with several pros about the special home details their clients are falling for—and what it takes to score your own home ripe with vintage appeal in a competitive market.

A renewed appreciation for period details—think original windows, layered molding, and aged floors—is something Peter Michael, founder of Piere Michel Brokerage, is witnessing firsthand.

“People are drawn to homes that feel lived in and loved,” he says. “Original craftsmanship is what this new wave of buyers is really looking for. It starts with the arches in the hallways to the exposed brick fireplaces—the list can go on and on.”

After years of streamlined interiors and minimalist façades, buyers are once again searching for texture, depth, and evidence of the human hand. “Original hardwood floors, high ceilings, stained glass, exposed brick, and vintage light fixtures are usually the things that pop out,” Michael adds. “They make a space feel rooted, not manufactured.”

That nostalgia isn’t confined to historic properties. Jackie Mosher, cofounder and COO of Dzinly, says even new builds are embracing time-worn charm through exterior details that nod to the past.

“I’m seeing a growing number of new homes incorporating decorative eaves and exposed rafters,” she says. “These details, once hallmarks of early Craftsman homes, are being reinterpreted today on Modern and Transitional houses. They add depth and visible craftsmanship to otherwise simple designs—bridging the gap between timeless tradition and contemporary simplicity.”

Mosher notes that stucco, once reserved for homes in warmer climates, is reemerging in cooler regions. “It’s fascinating to see homeowners in the Midwest and Northeast choosing stucco—it requires more effort to install because of the temperature fluctuations, but people are willing to make that investment for the aesthetic,” she explains. And while the material palette may draw from the past, the execution is crisp: thinner window frames, smooth finishes, and clean horizontal lines inspired by midcentury design.

The goal, Mosher says, isn’t replication—it’s reinterpretation. “People aren’t trying to recreate the past, but to blend nostalgic details with modern forms,” she explains. “That’s what makes a home feel truly timeless.”

Of course, today’s buyers want the best of both worlds. “They want the vintage look, but they can’t live without modern technology,” Michael says. “A smart thermostat is almost a must, along with stainless steel appliances. It’s a blend of things.”

That blend extends to floor plans as well. While classic Tudors, Craftsman bungalows, and brownstones are perpetually in demand, interior layouts are evolving to suit modern lifestyles. “Popcorn ceilings and small, isolated kitchens are becoming a no-no,” Michael says. “Buyers love a good open layout and want a clean-cut look with a mixture of craftsmanship.”

It’s that mixture—the contrast of the hand-hewn with the high-tech—that defines the moment.

Mosher believes the secret to timeless appeal lies in restraint. “For a home to feel enduring, the design shouldn’t lean too heavily on trends or overly bold choices,” she says. “Muted, nature-based tones—warm whites, taupes, and sages—create that sense of heritage. And contrasting the trim with the cladding helps define the home, grounding it in a way that feels classic.”

After decades of neutral sameness, it seems buyers are once again craving personality. They want arches instead of corners, warmth instead of gloss, and craftsmanship that invites you to look twice.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6863. Zendaya Recreated an Iconic Halle Berry Fashion Moment for Halloween

Zendaya didn’t miss out on all the fun this Halloween. The actress dressed up as one of Hollywood’s most enduring screen and style icons, Halle Berry, thanks to a special vintage pull.

On Saturday, Zendaya’s longtime stylist, Law Roach, shared a series of photos of the Dune actress recreating a particularly memorable early-aughts look that Berry wore: a butter yellow, brown, and white printed string bikini top with silver accents and matching low-rise, wide-leg trousers from Lanvin’s fall 2000 collection. Zendaya completed the look with pointed-toe heels.

Roach thanked Lanvin’s artistic director Peter Copping and Alix Long in his post. Copping took the creative reins at the French fashion house in 2024, and Cristina Ortiz was Lanvin’s womenswear designer when the fall 2000 collection debuted.

Berry first wore the two-piece to the New York premiere of X-Men in July 2000. She accessorized with a belly chain and tinted sunglasses.

Berry saw the tribute and posted her approval on X. “She won,” Berry wrote of Zendaya.

The fashion homage feels particularly fitting given that Zendaya has been drawing comparisons to Berry since debuting her pixie cut.

In fact, when Zendaya first unveiled the look at the 2026 Emmy Awards in September, Roach confirmed that the haircut was inspired by Berry’s iconic short hairstyle.

Berry later embraced the tribute, sharing Roach’s interview on her Instagram Story and writing that Zendaya “murdered the night.”

This isn’t the first time Zendaya has used fashion to pay tribute to another star. In 2013, she channeled the late Aaliyah for Keep a Child Alive’s Dream Halloween, recreating the singer’s iconic Tommy Hilfiger campaign look. More recently, she honored Cher at the 2024 Rock & Roll Hall of Fame induction ceremony in a vintage Bob Mackie design.

Zendaya and Roach have more fashion moments ahead: The actress’s Dune: Part Three press tour is just kicking off.

#6858. Why Everyone Is Wearing Suede This Fall—And the Chicest Ways to Style It

When the temperatures drop and fall dressing takes center stage, one fabric always finds its way back into our wardrobes: suede. Suede has been that fall fashion staple ever since it has been around. It brings that cozy, elevated feel to even the simplest outfits, making it the perfect texture to embrace when the seasons change.

While it’s common to see suede generally in the fall, lately it’s absolutely everywhere, and I’m here to remind you that there are so many ways you can wear this texture. From suede mini skirts paired with oversized sweaters, knee-high suede boots, and of course purses.

Suede is such a perfect fall trend because it brings a subtle touch of texture to any outfit. Shades like chocolate brown and deep burgundy make the material feel especially autumnal.

Consider this your sign to give your fall wardrobe a suede refresh. Whether you go all in with a statement skirt or keep things simple with a chic suede bag, this is one trend that makes every outfit feel a little more luxurious. And if you need some inspiration, I’ve got you covered with some trendy looks below.

There is something so effortlessly chic about a suede skirt. It’s a fun piece I’d wear to work, but styled like the above makes for such a cute day or night time look. The addition of the colored tights and shoes elevates the whole moment, so if you’re a maximalist this suede look is for you.

The simplest way to tap into the suede trend is with your footwear. There are so many popular suede shoes meant for the fall and winter season and these boots are the perfect ones.

Even though I am not a big hat girl, a suede hat like this is a fun way to test the waters. It’s easy and adds to any fall outfit you’re building.

Another staple way to tap into suede is with accessories, specifically a good purse. This dark brown suede shoulder bag is so stylish and paired with the all-white outfit is a texture combo I’m obsessing over.

Of course, no fall wardrobe is complete without a statement jacket. Suede blazers, like the one above, add instant personality to your everyday looks. They’re the kind of investment pieces that make getting dressed feel exciting, even on the busiest mornings.

#6856. The Best-Dressed Guest’s Guide to the Holiday Wedding Season

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Great dresses, fabulous accessories, and plenty of reasons to RSVP yes.

Gorgeous gowns, sleek stilettos, sparkling jewelry—there’s plenty to love about getting glammed up for a wedding. It’s a chance to have a little more fun with your fashion, whether that means wearing a more dramatic gown than you’d typically go for, trying a bold color rather than your usual neutrals, or finally breaking out those festive heels. Whether the invitation calls for a destination, beachfront ceremony or a cosmopolitan, black-tie celebration, there’s an art to dressing for the occasion while embracing your personal style.

And that’s why Bloomingdale’s is your go-to. From romantic florals and sweeping gowns to metallic heels, bold jewelry, and conversation-starting bags, there’s something for every kind of celebration. Ahead, 11 wedding guest finds so good you may even want to wear them for the holidays.

Moody florals feel especially right for a fall wedding, and Simkhai’s draped maxi matches the romance of a countryside estate or garden ceremony. This fluid, body-skimming silhouette is especially elegant; play up the deeper colors with burgundy heels and gold jewelry, or add a tailored blazer if there’s a chill in the air.

Make your bag the focal point with Cult Gaia’s sculptural olive-green clutch, complete with a wavy gold handle. The earthy color feels especially fitting for an outdoor wedding; pick up a floral-print dress in a similar shade, or pair it with butter yellow for a more unexpected color combination.

If there’s one shoe to have in your wedding-guest wardrobe, it’s a great gold sandal. Aqua’s strappy pair adds extra sparkle with crystal-trimmed buckles and a sculptural heel, making them a flawless match for everything from printed midis to black-tie gowns.

An asymmetric, off-the-shoulder neckline feels unexpected. The draped silhouette and uneven hem give it extra movement for a cocktail affair, especially when paired with sculptural earrings and barely-there heels.

A beach wedding calls for something a little more shell-ebratory. Simon Miller’s polished silver clutch has a playful seaside touch thanks to its sculptural shell shape and black tassel. It stands out against a silky slip dress or bold black gown.

Wedding guest dressing is the perfect excuse to bring out the statement earrings. Pair these BaubleBar chunky gold-and-blue studs with a simple one-shoulder dress for a minimalist cocktail look.

A black-tie invitation is your chance to fully send it, and this Amur gown delivers with dusty mauve silk, delicate lace appliqués, and romantic off-the-shoulder detailing. For an estate wedding or grand ballroom reception, add crystal earrings, a satin clutch, and a sleek updo to show off the neckline.

Loeffler Randall’s gathered leather design with gold ring detail keeps things understated but still has plenty of personality. Carry it with an evening gown or tailored suit for the big day, then bring it back out with jeans and a blazer for dinner.

A black-tie wedding invites a little sparkle. Jennifer Behr’s crystal-covered hoops bring the shine with a bold, rounded silhouette. Wear them with a strapless satin dress or a sharply tailored evening look, sweep your hair back, and skip the necklace to let the earrings catch everyone’s eye.

A printed floral slip dress is a stunning choice for a tropical destination wedding—especially one with an ocean view. Farm Rio’s fluid maxi looks beautiful with gold sandals and playful earrings.

Leave it to Jimmy Choo to make black heels feel anything but basic! With sheer floral lace, a pointed peep-toe, and a slender stiletto heel, these satin mules look especially gorgeous with a sleek column gown or silk midi for a formal evening wedding.

#6854. Winter Date Ideas to Heat Up Your Love Life This Season

Time to turn those dark, cold nights into hot date nights.

I’m afraid the rumors are true: Winter is, in fact, upon us. But while the colder weather may find you ready to cuddle up with takeout and TV ‘til spring, let this be your reminder that winter does not mean you have to put your dating life on hold. While this may not be the sexiest time of year, it is the coziest—which means that the cooler months ahead are filled with romantic date-night potential. To help you make the most of that potential, allow me to present this list of the best winter date ideas for you and whoever you’re cuddling up to this season.

Whether you’re already happily cuffed and committed, single and seeking, or feeling things out with a new crush, there’s something on this list for every kind of winter date vibe. Prepare to fill your cold-weather calendar with these 18 winter date ideas.

What better way to heat up your winter nights than an evening of laughs with the one you love? And by the way, you don’t have to shell out for a Netflix Special taping (although if you can swing it, by all means). Tons of venues in cities across the country regularly host affordable standup shows where you can grab some food or a drink and enjoy a chill night filled with plenty of laughs.

To be clear, you can support the arts any time. But there’s just something a little fancy-yet-cozy about winter ballet.

In a similar if much sexier vein, nothing beats a burlesque date night. If you’re New York–based (or have an NYC trip coming up this holiday season) I literally can’t recommend Company XIV’s Nutcracker Rouge highly enough. Get tickets here!

Can you technically see a play any time of year? Yes, of course. But you can’t tell me there’s not something about “going to the theater” that just seems to ooze a certain wintery sophistication. Whether it’s a Broadway show or a more intimate performance at your local theater, catching some live entertainment is a perfect way to spend a winter night out on the town.

If you’re down to class things up a bit this winter, nothing will make you feel more sexy and sophisticated than a jazz club date night—just you and your date, sipping cocktails and enjoying some live tunes.

If you and your person are already seasoned slope-hitters, then a ski trip is a given. But if you don’t know your way around the slopes yet, why not take a lesson together? It’s a great opportunity to learn something new, get outside, and make the most of the cold weather—and it comes with a built-in excuse to warm up with a steaming mug of hot chocolate (or your après-ski beverage of choice) afterwards.

Sometimes an intimate evening with a home-cooked meal you prepare together is actually more romantic than dining out. Pick a recipe you both want to try, open a bottle of wine, and channel your inner chef.

Plenty of companies now offer virtual wine tastings you can do from the comfort of your own home. They send the wine to you, and you and your date can follow along as an expert guides you through the various selections in a Zoom-style seminar. What better way to spend a night in than exploring new wines together and refining your sommelier sensibilities?

Alexa, play “Christmas Tree Farm” by Taylor Swift. Whether or not you actually want to perform the manual labor of cutting down a live tree, lugging it home, and setting it up when you could just order a fake from Amazon, spending a cozy day at a Christmas tree farm is an iconic holi-date. It’s outdoorsy without being too physically demanding, it’s brimming with cute selfie potential, and you can basically pretend you’re in the cheesy-yet-addictive holiday rom-com of your dreams. What more could you ask for?

Get into the holiday spirit and cross some seasonal chores off your to-do list by turning holiday decorating into a cute date night. Best of all: You get to stay inside and keep your comfy clothes on.

This one may sound a little less fun, but hear me out. Un-decorating is sad and boring and work—but turning it into date night can make an otherwise tedious chore actually kind of fun! Grab some wine, throw on a movie or set the mood with some music, and un-deck the halls.

While some of your summertime haunts may be closed for the season, plenty of typically summery destinations are actually open year-round. Not to mention, a lot of them are probably less expensive and less crowded during the off-season. Plan a trip to your favorite beach town or seaside locale and see what it’s like in the winter. Chances are it’s probably a lot more peaceful.

Spending a night together in a hotel is a great way to get out of the house and treat yourselves to a romantic mini-getaway. Some hotels even offer special holiday/winter packages for the season.

Nothing says cuffing season like meeting the parents! But even if you’re not ready to take that step, a little trip to your hometown to show your crush all the old haunts is a great way to spend a winter weekend and indulge in a little nostalgia.

Sometimes it can be tough to work up the motivation to actually get up and go out in the winter. It’s dark, it’s cold, your seasonal depression is acting up, etc. But that’s exactly why you deserve to treat yourselves to a luxe night out. You know that fancy restaurant you’ve been saving for a special occasion? Screw the special occasion. Go now, for no reason other than you can.

What better way to unwind and beat the winter blues than a spa date? Treat yourselves to a couple’s massage, a relaxing sauna steam, and/or some beauty treatments that your dry winter skin will definitely thank you for.

Lots of bars offer mixology classes where you and your date can not only enjoy some delicious seasonal cocktails but also learn to make them yourselves. If you’d rather not leave the house, there are also virtual options you can attend online from the comfort of your own home.

Going to the movies is a lost art. Catch a new release (Wuthering Heights, anyone?) and enjoy your date’s company while snacking on popcorn, candy, and slurpees

#6853. I Went to the World's Biggest Furniture Trade Show—These 6 Trends Are About to Be Everywhere

The furniture profiles, patterns, and palettes you’re about to see everywhere.

Twice a year, High Point, North Carolina, becomes the home industry’s giant showroom, where furniture and design brands unveil new collections for retailers, interior designers, and buyers to see and source. At House Beautiful, we’re your boots on the ground, looking for the ideas that may travel from showroom to store—and into your home next.

This fall, Market made a case for decorating with more character and muddier hues: green moved from the forest floor to the showroom floor, florals claimed entire sofas, and upholstery played with everything from a flash of leg to a full fabric wrap. Add wood-backed furniture as an accent, garden-inspired lighting, and a generous swish of fringe, and the message was clear: The details are ready to take center stage.

This year, green was the unofficial color of Market. Benjamin Moore and Behr had already put the hue on the radar with their 2027 Colors of the Year: Webster Green, a confident mid-tone balancing blue’s coolness with yellow’s warmth, and Grounded, a mossy olive softened by earthy brown undertones. Then came the showroom echoes. Versions of green, from earthy vegetable shades to deeper, moodier tones, appeared nearly everywhere. Kathy Kuo even created a “Music Room” (shown in our opening image) for Rowe Furniture inspired by the verdant shade. The appeal is easy to understand: Green brings the natural world indoors, lending a sense of calm and grounding a space without draining it of personality.

The design industry’s obsession with prints is now a love letter to botanicals. Florals erupted across Market in a myriad of scales, from small, almost sun-bleached botanicals to overscaled, tapestry-adjacent blooms. Even the tiniest prints made a surprisingly bold statement when carried across an entire sofa; the larger blooms brought the feel of a faded heirloom into full view. Both have that grandmotherly, another-era quality, but the impact is anything but timid. Yes, it’s a major design commitment, but why play it safe with a solid, barely-there pattern? It may not be for the faint of design, but we are all in a sofa fully dressed in flowers.

Here’s a furniture detail that changes the whole silhouette: Brands are letting wood take over the outside back—or sides—of upholstered seating. Baker’s sofa makes the move hard to miss, with a broad wood shell framing the piece. It’s a little like “inside-out” upholstery, where the outside gets its own treatment, but here the wood gives the piece architectural shape and presence. As floor plans open up and sofas float in family and living rooms, the back is no longer hidden against a wall. It’s part of the view, and part of how the seating area takes shape, making it all the more important that it feels worth looking at.

Upholstery continues to take its cues from fashion, with a focus on mixing silhouettes: Mini skirts that let those gams show; full, ballooning skirts with all the best parts of an ’80s prom dress; and what we lovingly call body-con upholstery, where the entire chair is wrapped head to toe in one fabric.

The appeal goes beyond a good fashion metaphor, though. Each treatment changes the chair’s proportions: a flash of leg keeps it light, a generous skirt adds volume, and an all-over wrap turns both seat and base into one continuous form, with no leg finish to choose or worry about. Each has its own distinct style POV, making your shopping choices that much more complicated, but in the best way.

If garden motifs aren’t your thing, don’t look up. The floral revival isn’t limited to upholstery and wallpaper; it’s reaching the ceiling, too. Visual Comfort & Co.’s Fairoaks collection, designed by Fisher Weisman, includes a chandelier, sconce, and mini flush mount. Sculptural branches and twigs cleverly disguise the chandelier’s ceiling cord, preserving the romance of the design. At Hudson Valley Lighting, a tulip-inspired chandelier brings a more literal bloom overhead. Both offer a way to bring the garden indoors through shape and silhouette, without committing an entire room to pattern.

Sometimes, all it takes is a little bullion fringe. The thick, tightly twisted cords fall in a dense, weighty row, adding plush texture and a satisfying swish to a skirted chair or sofa. It nods to the Victorian parlor, but stops short of period-drama excess; against a cleaner silhouette or in a spare room, the old-world trim feels playful and unexpectedly fresh. Consider it the design equivalent of a great pair of earrings: a little extra, in exactly the right place.

#6852. Joseph Zada and Mckenna Grace Have Thoughts About Fans Shipping Them Off-Screen

The co-stars have seen your fancams—and they’re setting the record straight.

Mckenna Grace and Joseph Zada may play acquaintances-turned-District 12 tributes in The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping, but that hasn’t stopped fans from shipping them in real life.

Well, the co-stars have seen all those fancams and social media threads, and they recently addressed those hoping their on-screen chemistry translates to something romantic IRL.

The actors debunked any notion that they’re anything more than good friends, fully bursting into laughter when Refinery29 Australia asked how they felt about people shipping them off-screen.

“It’s pretty weird,” Joseph admitted, before adding, “Mckenna does feel like my younger, younger sister.”

Mckenna also chimed in, saying, “Joe’s like family. I mean…I think that’s the fun of the internet, is that they can ship whatever characters or people or anything that they want, as long as it’s not intruding on people’s own lives.”

“Yeah, I’ve been shipped with Ben [Wang],” Joe jokingly added, referring to another one of their castmates.

The pair have long maintained that they’re just friends—not to mention that, as Joseph says in the video, he and Mckenna have “both got partners” IRL. Who is Joseph’s partner, you ask? That would be Polly Talarico, who he met in a bar in Sydney earlier this year, according to his recent interview with Vanity Fair.

Mckenna is currently linked to her “best friend” and Regretting You co-star, Mason Thames, who she gushed about during her Cosmopolitan cover story when she was asked about her love life off-screen.

“Your girl is happy. I’m feeling very lovey-dovey,” she shared. “I have somebody I can be a complete nerd with. I got my best friend, and so I don’t see the point in not loving loudly.”

She added that she and Mason are hanging out on the sets of each other’s projects so much that “he’s like an honorary member of District 12 at this point.”

“There are moments that are just for you and your person. But I don’t see the point in not celebrating who you’re in love with,” she said of their public relationship. “A lot of people’s hesitation around this stems from the idea that if you post about someone a bunch and then you break up, that’s embarrassing. But why would I even think about that? I’m just going to focus on the present.”

#6851. Ralph Lauren’s Polofest Was a Fashion Obsessive’s Dream

As the brand approaches 60, a three-day multidimensional experience brought together passionate fans from around the world

Few brands carry a sense of fandom and lore like Ralph Lauren. Followers of the brand approach it with a disciple-like devotion. They collect the clothes, swap stories, build kinship and community globally, and embrace a Polo way of life. To mark the brand’s 60th anniversary, Ralph Lauren recently assembled fans, collectors, and sellers for Polofest, its inaugural festival celebrating all things Polo in the heart of Shibuya, Tokyo.

Polofest was a three-day event celebrating the brand’s history and its deep connection with Japan from the people who know it best. The festival saw celebrities side by side with vintage Polo collectors rifling through rails looking for rare and archival Polo items brought there by 17 of the world’s biggest Ralph Lauren sellers. Brand enthusiasts and cultural voices shared the stage on panels that discussed topics like The Cult of Collecting and Why Tokyo Matters. Vintage photographs of Ralph Lauren that span decades of him visiting and working in Japan were shown as part of a mini exhibition at the festival’s entrance. The event was the first in a series of global activations that will celebrate Ralph Lauren’s impact on style, culture, and lifestyle over the next year.

Japan’s love of Americana dates back to the 1960s, when Japanese youth subcultures—most notably a Ginza–based style tribe known as the Miyuki-Zoku—began hanging around Tokyo’s Miyuki-dori, one of the city’s big shopping streets. They were obsessed with American collegiate fashion—button-down shirts and tailored blazers—long before Ralph Lauren officially landed.

But when the brand did land, in 1976, opening the first Polo Ralph Lauren store in partnership with department store Seibu, it was an immediate hit. Japanese fashion obsessives were taken with the robust construction, fabric aging, and inbuilt patina that was expected from American garments. Then in the 1980s, the look evolved into the Kir Kaji (clean casual) movement, where double-breasted blue blazers, tennis sweaters, and, of course, the Polo shirt became emblematic of a preppy American look that Japan was keen to emulate.

Closer to home, Brooklyn had given birth to the Lo-Lifes, a movement of kids who were such fans of Ralph Lauren that they often shoplifted items if they couldn’t afford them. (The “Lo” in their name was, of course, taken from the word “Polo.”) This passionate fandom helped cement the hype around the brand in the 1980s and 1990s—hype that has endured until today.

Even as the brand has expanded Lauren’s work beyond its preppy roots to include his varied visions of American life—western, streetwear, collegiate, prep, and beyond—the fandom around him has only grown. Many can be clothiers, but very few can lay claim to creating an entire culture and community around their work.

OutKast’s André 3000, who attended Polofest and sat with Esquire’s Michael Sebastian in conversation, noted, “Ralph Lauren wasn’t necessarily from the world that he ended up creating. If you are not from it, you want to make it the best. So I think other cultures, they look at America and they love it so much—they love rock ’n’ roll so much, they love rap so much—that they care for it in a certain way, and that’s how you have Ralph Lauren. You gotta have somebody who loves it.”

Watch below as Bazaar’s executive digital director Lynette Nylander reports on the ground from Polofest.

#6848. Hailey Bieber and Zoë Kravitz Are Loving This Low-Maintenance Nail Trend for Fall

Here’s how to recreate the goes-with-anything manicure.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Last night’s CFDA Awards brought all of fashion’s coolest people together. With glitzy designer dresses and a star-studded carpet, the event boasted a guest list with A-listers like Kim Kardashian, Demi Moore, Megan Thee Stallion, and more, at the Museum of Natural History in New York City. But amid the luxe labels and prestigious awards, one beauty trend was the common denominator taking over the carpet: glassy nails.

In a sea of fall nail colors like earthy greens and moody burgundies, glassy manicures offer a welcome break from bolder polishes. Celebrity beauty muses like Hailey Bieber, Zoë Kravitz, and more were spotted rocking the barely-there manicure. And the best part of all? The sheer, glossy finish goes with just about everything, making it an easy look to wear all season (or year) long, no matter what you're wearing.

This sheer manicure is so easy to DIY, and it’s also super low-maintenance. As it starts to grow out, unlike a bright or deep hue, you can barely tell where the new growth begins.

Start by grooming and shaping your nails for a clean base. Trim any loose cuticles, buff away uneven texture, and file them into your desired shape. Whether you like your fingertips short and round, or long and almond, this fresh finish looks good on any length (as showcased by the chic celebrities who have been rocking many versions of the look lately).

Once your canvas is prepped and clean, choose your glass polish. You can lean a little milkier and pinker with a shade like Essie’s Skinny Dip, or opt for a nourishing formula like Manucurist’s Active Bright. Or, go for a completely shiny finish with a clear coat of OPI’s Start to Finish. For a more translucent look, use just one coat of your desired polish, and for more opaque finishes, two layers should do the trick.

Jasmine Hyman is the Assistant Beauty Editor at Cosmopolitan, where she writes about the latest beauty trends and must-have products. Her most prized beauty possessions are a meticulous skincare routine and salon blowouts. You’ll also likely find her in bed reading a good book or endlessly scrolling TikTok (spoiler: it’s usually the latter) while listening to Harry Styles’ entire discography on repeat. Follow her on Insta to be inundated with pictures of her meals.

#6847. The Royals Share a Throwback Photo of Prince Louis for Halloween

Happy Halloween! Today, as people all over the world dress up as ghosts or witches or vampires or their favorite pop stars, the royal family celebrated the spooky holiday with a sweet autumnal throwback. Taking to Instagram, Prince William and Princess Kate shared a photo of a one-year-old Prince Louis, pulled from their own personal archives.

Photographed by Kate herself, the image showed the youngest Wales child—now eight years old—with a pumpkin, perhaps about to attempt to pick it up. The toddler was bundled up in a blue coat, layered over a blue-and-white plaid shirt. His red boots were muddy from a day outside and his cheeks were rosy from the cold. (And while he might be known for his sassy facial expressions, here, he was all smiles.)

The prince and princess have long prioritized a life surrounded by nature, often raising awareness about the positive impacts of spending time outside and how we can help protect the environment for future generations. Just last week, on Mountain Rescue Awareness Day, the couple posted a photo of the whole family at the base of the Three Peaks Challenge—one of the United Kingdom’s most challenging outdoor endurance events, in which participants climb the highest mountains in Scotland, England, and Wales within 24 hours. Kate completed the challenge back in June to raise funds for the Royal Marsden Cancer Charity, and revisited the milestone to thank the local mountain rescue teams “for the vital role they play in keeping walkers and climbers safe on the mountains all year round.”

#6846. This Unexpected Antique Is Making a Major Comeback—and It Might Be Hiding in Your Grandma’s Art Collection

This centuries-old style brings fresh character to the walls.

Vintage art adds soul to a space. There’s just something about that story and patina that lends itself to more personality for your walls. While oil paintings and tiny art have certainly had their time to shine, there’s another antique art form that’s taking center stage: They’re called reverse glass paintings.

Popular in India in the 18th and 19th centuries, reverse glass paintings are illustrations that have been hand-painted onto the back of glass, giving them a painterly, colorful look. Annalise Holasz, founder of vintage art gallery Hue + Holasz, says that these works have a “really naïve” quality since each is painted by hand, and the “little variations make each one special.”

Holasz also notes that their frames, often made from reclaimed wood, are particularly special, since they provide a charming, aged, and even battered appearance. These artworks can showcase visuals like flowers, plants, Indian princes, boats, and shells, as Holasz says, “but animals are probably the most common,” and include birds, fish, sea creatures, and jungle animals.

To learn more about reverse glass paintings, we tapped the experts, who are sharing what to look for when starting your own collection.

Alexander Widener, owner of Widener Company, believes that reverse glass paintings are rising in popularity because “they capture the spirit of craft and yesteryear that so many of us are craving in our increasingly digital world.” It also doesn’t hurt that earlier this year, designer Tory Burch gave Vogue a tour of her Paris apartment, where reverse glass paintings were spotlighted in her powder room.

“I think people are drawn to how fun and playful they are,” Holasz says. “There’s often a subject that feels personal, and the combination of vibrant color and a characterful wooden frame appeals.”

Melanie Lissack of Melanie Lissack Interiors believes that reverse glass paintings are trending because they are eclectic, unique, and vintage in style while still having a modern twist.

While current-day pieces can look remarkably similar to the bygone versions, Lissack thinks they just don’t carry the same charm as the antique pieces—as striking as they are. Those in the know don’t look at the artwork first; they look at the frame. For instance, Lissack is on the lookout for slightly worn frames.

Widener points out that if you’re questing after particularly old reverse glass paintings, you should look for bubbles in the glass, a sign that the glass was handblown. This was a standard practice up until the early 20th century.

Also, it can be as simple as buying the motifs you love. For Widener, it’s country scenes and images of animals. He’s also not put off if they don’t have a frame at all.

“If you aren’t heading to Jaipur anytime soon, then Etsy and eBay are great places to look,” Holasz shares. “Sellers restock weekly. I’d browse quite a few listings before choosing, because the frame can make such a difference. They can sell pretty quicky as they are always in demand though, so it’s worth doing some research to shortlist styles you like so you can move quickly when the right thing comes along.”

If you’re searching for the truly antique varieties (made in the 18th and 19th centuries), Holasz adds that these are likely more available at auctions.

Also, don’t count out flea markets and antique shops for these finds. For reverse glass paintings, according to current eBay listings, you can expect to pay as low as $5 all the way up to $5,000. The prices for these paintings tend to be a steal these days since they’re just starting to have a moment, so snatch them up before the prices rise.

While you can certainly give the spotlight to a single reverse glass painting, many designers believe these diminutive pieces look best when hung together in small groups.

As for rooms where these works look their best, Holasz thinks they look “really adorable” in children’s rooms, from nurseries to bedrooms to playrooms. They also act as hardy artwork that can be displayed in kitchens and baths, since, as Lissack says, the paintings are “protected by being on the rear side of the glass.”

Also, don’t be afraid to go a bit rogue with these pieces. For example, Holasz likes to mix them into a gallery wall alongside oil paintings, drawings, and sketches, since the different surfaces create visual interest. And you don’t have to hang them on the wall—they also look great leaned on bookshelves.

Widener, who often scores unframed versions, prefers to display them on wire plate hangers.

“When placed in a window, the light comes through in a really beautiful way,” he says.

#6845. I've Had This Modular Sofa for Four Years, and Its Secret Twin "Bed" Is a Hosting Game-Changer

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The first couch I ordered for my basement was a bust. No matter how hard they tried, the delivery men couldn’t angle it to get it down the narrow stairs. Because I live in a 1930s home, all the charm turns to chaos when it comes to fitting new furniture. I was literally stuck because a family of five was visiting for the holidays and they had nothing to sit (or sleep) on. Plus, what even is a basement without a comfy couch?

Ideally, I wanted a sleeper sofa to accommodate my guests and let my kids have sleepovers. But there was absolutely no way I would risk buying another couch that couldn’t come apart and was stuck in a corner. For round two, I knew that I needed a modular sectional. After a bit of research, I came across the Burrow Range sectional and was elated that it could be assembled like a building-block puzzle that can grow over time if I wanted to add more sections.

Since the basement isn’t huge, my selection was a four-piece model that included two arm pieces as well as an ottoman. Because my dreams of a sleeper were no more, I liked that the Range has flippable top cushions that, when pulled back, could make a twin-size bed. Happily, four years later, the sectional has survived many, many sleepovers and now resides in my bedroom. Read on to see how it transitioned from child-hopping to a peaceful nook two floors up.

Best For: A growing household or someone who likes to change the layout of their space often.

Not ideal for: Someone who is very tall. If you are taller than 6 feet, the sofa may sit too low for you.

Price at review (4-piece sectional lounger): $1,271 (originally $2,119)

Bottom Line: The modular sectional is comfortable and won’t sag or end up with flat cushions, even after years of use.

Because I needed the sectional before the winter holidays, I was glad that I could order by early December and still get my sofa before Santa came to town. The four big boxes for the cushions and a little box for legs came to my front door within a week.

My husband and I brought down all the boxes to the basement where we started to unbox and assemble. The overall process took no more than 30 minutes. In a nutshell, you need to line up the holes with the metal braces and secure those with large plastic knob screws. The cushions have two strips of Velcro that stick to the black fabric at the base to keep them from sliding out from beneath.

The sofa pieces are easy to secure together.

Compared to my living room couch, which is a mid-century modern style West Elm reversible sectional, the Burrow has a bit more bounce to the cushion. While the seats are softer, I would still describe it as medium-firm. As soon as I sit down, it feels relaxing. Because it is only 28 inches high, it is a little lower than I am used to. (This still works out because I can slouch down and stretch my feet up on the ottoman when I watch a movie.)

Later, when I moved the sectional up to my bedroom, I lie across all three seat cushions, with my head resting on the arm cushion, as if I am reading in bed. The arm cushions are slightly angled, with more padding at the bottom to create a gentle incline that supports my head.

I chose the Stone Gray upholstery, an almost salt-and-pepper look, in performance flatweave. This is my third woven upholstery sofa, and it is the softest. It feels similar to a worn-in tweed jacket. I think it actually got a bit softer with age. I am happy to report there is absolutely no pilling or loose threads.

My biggest pet peeve is saggy couch cushions. I cannot stand it when the fabric looks crumpled on the seats, and there are sloppy valleys on the back cushions. After having this sectional for four years, I can vouch that it still looks the same as the day we assembled it. Which is a small miracle because when the Range lived in the basement, it was base camp for elementary-age boys playing video games and jumping around. If for some reason you wanted to fluff, you can unzip the underside of the back cushions and take out the filling to give it more shape.

In terms of stains and spills, if any kids spilled something on the sofa, I don’t know about it because it has no markings. Come to think of it, I don’t think I’ve ever had to clean the sofa beyond brushing off crumbs.

I did catch my cat trying to stretch (scratch) on the back of the sectional a few times, but I shooed him away, and he did not make it a habit. When I checked, there were never any loops or snags. (I cannot say the same about another woven sofa that is a victim of his claws.)

The cushions are still in terrific shape.

The flexibility of the Burrow Range sectional is everything. That’s the main reason I would highly recommend this couch. Not only do the pieces come in individual boxes that can fit through narrow doorways of older homes, but you can customize the sectional to your exact needs.

Originally, I needed something to help with hosting my out-of-town family, but this sectional has proven again and again that it’s a high-quality piece that can hold up for years to come. I still can’t believe that the cushions are in tip-top shape.

Pricewise, it’s a solid buy for mid-range furniture, especially because I don’t see a reason to replace it any time soon. Even when I ended up getting a new green modular sectional to go with the new wallpaper that I installed during my basement makeover, I found a spot for it in my bedroom. The Range now serves as a reading nook or a makeshift WFH station when I don’t want to stay in bed but feel too lazy to walk down the stairs to my home office. I added a Häti pouf with a scalloped EtúHome board next to the Burrow ottoman, and it serves as a perch for my coffee.

All in all, I am very happy with this purchase. It is durable, comfortable, and boasts clean lines and a silhouette that is approachable, but a bit more interesting than a standard traditional sofa.

#6833. This Brand-New Illinois Farmhouse Was Designed to Look Hundreds of Years Old

The team at Buccellato Design and Architecture focused on incorporating heritage details and timeworn furnishings.

For Aimee and Kevin Buccellato, the best compliment they could receive about a project isn’t, “Wow, it looks so different in here.” It’s that a home looks like they haven’t changed a thing.

On a country road in Momence, Illinois, a beloved farmhouse stands as a timeworn landmark for the surrounding agricultural community. For the homeowners—a retired couple returning to their Midwestern roots—the property was as much a repository of memories as it was a place to create new ones. “Having grown up nearby, the property evoked a strong sense of memory and place,” says Aimee, who founded Buccellato Design and Architecture alongside her husband, Kevin. “At the outset of the project, and throughout, our clients wanted the new house to feel like it had always been there, so much so that perhaps the neighbors could be led to believe that the original house remained intact, yet amended by skillful renovation and discreet additions.”

It was an architectural sleight of hand rooted in a distinctly Midwestern philosophy: “Be good to the neighbors.” Alongside the Buccellatos, the couple approached the 6,000-square-foot rebuild with the restraint of preservationists and the imagination of storytellers. “Great care was taken to instill the home and outbuildings with a sense of history and rootedness, creating a home that feels as though it’s been part of this bucolic setting for centuries,” says Kevin of the project, which also included the restoration of the property’s original 19th-century barn and corn crib. “We drew inspiration from traditional farm complexes, where homesteads were added slowly over time, reconnecting the site with its Folk-Victorian-Farm-House and agricultural building roots.”

Inside, the narrative continues through antique white oak floors, salvaged beams, heritage florals, and rooms that borrow their palettes from the landscape. In the kitchen, a wall of windows replaces upper cabinetry, framing sunrise over the barn and walnut trees. The glass porch offers sweeping views of the Kankakee River, while a tucked-away speakeasy recalls the original farmhouse’s stone foundation.

For Kevin and Aimee, the guiding principle was a new construction home that felt “sensitive, not wasteful, polite, not ostentatious,” an exercise in knowing when architecture and interiors should defer to their surroundings. The result is a house with an invented past and a very real sense of belonging that does the Midwest—and the neighbors—proud.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Buccellato Design and ArchitectureLocation: Momence, IllinoisThe Space: A four-bedroom, four-and-a-half-bathroom home across 6,000 square feet

The entryway offers a fitting introduction to the home'’s carefully constructed sense of history, replete with nickel gap paneling, fumed and waxed antique white oak floors, and a light-filled staircase. Even the furnishings—including a vintage Persian rug and antique Jacobean gateleg table—suggest a collection acquired over generations.

Benjamin Moore’s Nelson Blue sets a serene backdrop for the sitting room, where traditional silhouettes and understated patterns make an easy pairing. “This room was designed to honor the history of this property and embody the love that our clients hold for this special place,” adds Aimee.

A tufted mohair sofa, realized in a rich umber, lends a sense of history, while an iconic feather lamp by Bunny Williams offers a reminder that even the most classic rooms benefit from a little irreverence.

In the kitchen, the Buccellatos traded upper cabinets for an expansive wall of windows overlooking the farmland, relocating the practical necessities to an adjacent dish room and larder. Salvaged white oak beams and barnboards overhead reinforce the home’s agricultural roots, while furniture-like cabinetry and leathered black quartzite countertops strike a timeless note.

With natural light pouring in from three sides, the dining room is what the Buccellatos describe as “peaceful, soft, and ethereal,” equally suited to both morning coffee and candlelit dinners. A dreamy mural wallpaper by Susan Harter references the property’s location along the Kankakee River, while formal seating lends a sense of occasion without sacrificing the home’s easygoing country charm.

Nestled among mature trees, the primary bedroom feels almost suspended in the surrounding landscape. The Buccellatos leaned into that sense of serenity with the palette. “It’s a dreamlike mix of earthy neutrals and pale blues that paints a serene backdrop for elegant details, vintage furnishings, and a delicate mix of subtle pattern,” adds Kevin.

A French country canopy bed, dressed in tailored Schumacher linen, takes center stage, while vintage-inspired furnishings and subtle pattern keep the room from feeling overly precious. An adjacent sitting room offers a place to read the morning paper or unwind with a cup of tea in the evenings.

In the vestibule, a little pattern goes a long way. A climbing floral from Schumacher in inky charcoal makes a charming counterpoint to the bedroom’s hushed palette, while a checked window bench is the ideal spot to read, giving the passageway purpose.

Pale blue walls and creamy millwork lend the primary bathroom an old-fashioned prettiness. A custom vanity introduces a tailored architectural element, while a Cowtan & Tout floral Roman shade softens the room’s more utilitarian details.

Designed as a playful respite for guests and future grandchildren, this bedroom plays off pattern and primary hues. “The red painted woodwork feels unexpected and cheerful against the blue-and-white wallpaper, striped rug, and patterned bedding,” says Aimee.

“The house and barn are nestled within nature and encircled by old-growth trees that offer a sense of place and belonging,” says Kevin of the home’s idyllic backdrop.

Founded by Aimee and Kevin Buccellato, Buccellato Design & Architecture is a practice marked by twenty-plus years of close collaboration, in architectural practice and in life. With projects on both coasts and throughout the country, the firm’s award-winning work reveals a dedication to craft, artistry, and detail. Achieved in partnership with clients, their projects reflect a purposeful sense of rootedness in the landscape and abiding devotion to beauty and the comfort of home.

#6825. Beyoncé Shares a Rare Look at Blue Ivy Carter’s Casual Style in a New Family Photo

Earlier this month, Blue Ivy Carter joined her father, Jay-Z, during night one of his Jay-Z 30 Tour in Los Angeles, playing piano for “The Story of O.J.” and “Dead Presidents.” While the 14-year-old didn’t take the stage for night two, she was still at the show, supporting him.

Beyoncé recently posted personal family photos of the night on her site, including a shot of Blue with her and her mother, Tina Knowles. Blue is dressed casually, wearing a striped jersey with shorts and tan boots. Beyoncé, meanwhile, opted for a LaPointe look from the brand’s spring/summer 2027 collection.

Last week, Beyoncé also posted photos from night one of Jay-Z’s Los Angeles tour stop on her site, where she included another shot of Blue with her and Knowles. The three women held hands backstage.

Blue is eager to perform, her dad told GQ back in March. He reflected on her musical talent, saying, “Blue is a crazy pianist, but she won’t let us get her a teacher. She doesn’t want it to be a job. But she has perfect pitch. If she hears a song, she’ll be like ‘Play it again’ and then she’ll teach herself. That’s just talent, she doesn’t work at that. She worked at this [being a dancer on Beyoncé’s tours], and it makes me proud that she fought for something that she really wanted to do. I don’t think we’re going to be able to get her off that stage now.”

Beyoncé also spoke about her eldest daughter’s interest in performing during her 2024 GQ interview. “Blue is an artist,” she began. “She has great taste in music and fashion. She is a fantastic editor, painter, and actress. She has been creating characters since she was 3. She’s a natural, but I did not want Blue onstage [dancing during my tours]. Blue wanted it for herself. She took it seriously and she earned it. And most importantly, she had fun! We all watched her grow more and more every night before our eyes.”

#6819. 33 Gifts for Wives Who Hope You've Been Listening

One of the great pleasures of being married is bearing witness to your life partner's tastes and obsessions. And you'd think all that inside knowledge would make buying her a gift a cinch, and yet here you are, looking up the best gifts for wives. No judgment—we've been there. Whether it's an anniversary, a birthday, or just an occasion to remind her how much you appreciate her, we've rounded up a few things that have been on our radar all year. And yes, you should probably make that dinner reservation, too.

Still got names on your list? We've got ideas. Peruse the rest of our gift guides, and sign up for the GQ Recommends newsletter for a regular dispatch of things we think are worth your money.

It's easy to get distracted by all the excellent cashmere at Banana, but the brand knows its way around silk, too. This shirt is cut roomy and relaxed, with the sort of effortless drape that makes getting dressed easy.

Coach's Empire Carryall comes in a whole family of sizes, all cut from the brand's soft glovetanned leather. It's a beautiful bag on its own, but I'd suggest picking out a few charms to dangle from the handles to make it hers.

Phoebe Philo's Peak sunglasses are aptly named: the shape is, quite simply, peak. An absolute stunner of a pair, and just imagine her walking into brunch wearing them.

Depending on whom you ask, the Moon Shoe might just be Nike's defining sneaker of the moment. Credit Jacquemus, whose collaboration turned a relic of the brand's running history into one of fashion's most coveted shoes.

J.Crew has barn jackets on the brain this year, and its considerable efforts might just have you lusting after one yourself. I'm partial to the studded version, but there's plenty to choose from, including corduroy and faux shearling renditions.

A silk scarf is an endlessly good gift, if only because there are endless ways to wear one. Around her neck, in her hair, knotted onto a handbag, however she pleases, really.

Brooches are all over the runways right now, and might just be the easiest way to lend an outfit a little extra panache. This dangly little number from Lie Studio features carnelian stone, with a rich, brownish-red hue that feels of the moment.

A coworker Chris first put me onto Simuero, but it wasn't until a trip to Barcelona earlier this year that I got to see the Spanish jewelry label's work in person, at Bassal Store. Beautiful stuff, with an organic, slightly imperfect quality that's even better up close.

This is about as chic as it gets, as far as under-eye patches go. The silky patches promise a little firming power, and the set also includes the Le Lift hand cream as an extra treat.

Makeup, of course, is a tricky thing to gift, but a colleague of mine showed up to the office wearing this purple blush from Violette_FR, and I was immediately sold on the shade. I've had it on my running list of recommendations ever since.

Soft Services has shrunk its beloved exfoliating Buffing Bar down to miniature proportions, with four little bars in a very giftable set. A posh way to keep skin in check, and low-key great for travel.

There was plenty of oohing and aahing when a small sample of Maison Francis Kurkdjian's Oud Velvet Mood landed on my desk this year. A gorgeous oud with notes of peach, suede, and leather.

Dr. Sturm has built quite a following for her science-minded approach to skincare. This travel-friendly set brings together some of her signature formulas, all tucked into a fuzzy fleece bag.

Sidia's Braless Duo pairs a subtly scented mist and serum, a welcome salve for winter-weary skin.

Rocco's game-changing beverage fridge now has a more affordable sibling. The Essential holds more than 100 cans and costs considerably less than the flagship Super Smart Fridge, while retaining the same compressor.

One floral and bittersweet, the other peachy and citrus-forward, these botanical aperitivi are deliciously complex without trying to taste like booze. Serve over ice or topped with soda.

Caraway isn't the first to make a coffee maker with an all-glass-and-steel brewing system, keeping hot coffee away from plastic. But it might just be the best-looking of the bunch, which counts when it's one of the first things they see in the morning.

What, your dinner parties don't involve serving salad with horn-handled, mother-of-pearl spoons? Gohar's nautilus shell-inspired salad servers are spectacularly unnecessary, which is why they make such a great gift.

Hario might be best known for its coffee gear, but the Japanese brand knows its way around matcha, too. This gift set includes a double-walled glass bowl and everything she needs to prepare a prower bowl of matcha.

Just the thing to christen that new matcha set. Kettl sources its teas directly from growers, and I'm currently taken with its Okuju matcha. Developed with a longtime partner in Uji, Japan's storied tea-growing region just outside Kyoto, it's a particularly fine powder with a delicate nuttiness and a wonderfully smooth finish.

Edie Parker's gemstoned lighter case has long been a go-to gift around these parts, but this year we've got our eye on the matching ashtray. No smoking equired—it makes a pretty fab catchall for jewelry, keys, and other little things.

I love the freaky pop-art vibes of this collaboration between Dusen Dusen and Venetian glassmaker Laguna~B. The pitcher is great on its own, but you can also spring for the set with two matching glasses.

Apple's new AirPods 5 come in two tiers, and the more giftable of the two is the version with the wireless charging case. You get longer battery life and swipe-to-adjust volume controls right on the stems.

For the record collector, U-Turn's Orbit Plus is one of the finest midrange turntables around. This version has a built-in phono preamp, so she can get straight to spinning records with a pair of powered speakers.

Taking its cues from Kodak's '70s-era 110 camera, Camp Snap offers a screen-free digital experience with an 8MP sensor, which is modest by today's standards, but entirely the point.

Nothing says great gift quite like an overengineered personal fan she'd never buy herself. Dyson's bladeless design is the clever bit, delivering a steady stream of air that's whisper-quiet.

If she's going to track her health, she might as well do it with the latest Oura Ring. The fifth-gen model is 40% smaller, with more proactive insights and personalized guidance to help her make sense of all that data.

Electric toothbrushes aren't exactly paragons of sustainable design, but Suri is doing its damnedest to change that. Its Pro 2.0 has recyclable plant-based brush heads, a repairable body, and a rechargeable battery designed to keep the whole thing out of a landfill for longer.

How could Theragun possibly improve on its already excellent mini massage gun, you ask? By adding heat, of course. The Mini Plus combines percussive massage with warming therapy to help loosen up sore muscles.

If cupping is good enough for the sporting elite, it's certainly worth a spot in her recovery routine. Wthn's kit includes eight silicone cups for a gentler take on the practice that can still help relieve muscle tension.

Wonder Valley's hinoki-scented bath products have won over a few of GQ's more relaxation-minded staffers. This set pairs a handsome bar of soap with a sachet of bath tea, which can be steeped a few times before it's finally spent.

Red-light technology is coming for sex toys, and Lelo's Mona Spectra is among the first to get in on the action. Expect to see plenty more of it in the years ahead, but there's nothing wrong with being an early adopter in this particular department.

#6817. Skyler Gisondo on His New Fashion Campaign—and Dressing Like Harrison Ford in the ’90s

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The Primetime star is gearing up in Bonobos and getting ready for a very big 2027.

Skyler Gisondo is remarkably easy to talk to. If you’ve seen him on one of your various screens—whether as Decoy Dan from Primetime, Jimmy Olsen from Superman, or maybe just that meme of him from a decade-old red-carpet photo—you probably assumed as much. It’s the big smile, the friendly eyes, the freckles, the general sense of wholesomeness. But I can confirm it, because even though I know we’ve got a relatively brief window of time to discuss his new campaign with men’s fashion brand Bonobos over Zoom, we spend the first few minutes of our chat just … shooting the shit. It feels like catching up with a buddy you haven’t seen in a while. Relaxed. Natural.

Thankfully, the vibe continues as we start digging into matters of style. Gisondo tells me he’s always been interested in dressing well but hasn’t always known where to start. “I would see a cool dude walking down the street or something, and think, ‘Man, I want to look like that guy.’ But I never knew how. I always felt lost,” he says. He describes his 20s as a decade of “desperately trying on shoes that didn’t fit or something.”

He turned 30 recently, though, and “I really feel like over the last couple of years, I’ve homed in on my sense of style, what I feel good and confident in.” A very good time, then, to star in a fashion campaign—especially considering this seems like a breakout moment for the actor. He’s been working consistently since he was a kid, but his performance in Primetime as a young actor who gradually realizes just how toxic the entertainment industry can be has clearly launched him to another level. And he still has his upcoming role in Focker-in-Law this November and a couple more appearances as Jimmy Olsen (in Man of Tomorrow on the big screen and The People v. Gorilla Grodd on television) on the docket for 2027.

Over the course of our conversation, we touch on everything from dressing like a cowboy (a mistake) to using Harrison Ford in the ’90s as your style North Star (a very good idea). We also chat about the stuff he’s stolen from sets for his own wardrobe, the psychological validation of joining the Shrek franchise, and why, after Primetime, we can expect more creative big swings in the future. Read on for a few highlights.

This interview has been condensed and edited.

“It’s ultimately about your own confidence. I feel like the sweet spot for fashion is when what you look like reflects how you feel inside—when you get to this equilibrium and it allows you to be a more fully realized version of yourself. We all grow and our sense of fashion evolves with us but ultimately like I feel like there should be some current that runs through all of it. The stuff I’m drawn to is the more timeless, classic stuff. I look at Harrison Ford in the ’90s and I’m just like, it doesn’t get cooler, you know?”

“For a while, for whatever reason, me and every actor I know were like trying to look like a cowboy. And it just hit me one day: ‘None of us have ever worked a day in our lives on a cattle ranch, man.’ I don’t even know how to use a hammer! Like, what are we doing? Can we just be ourselves?”

“I’m pretty active. I surf and hike a lot when I’m at home. And I love walking; if I have an hour or two between meetings, I’ll just walk a few miles and take calls. I want something comfortable and functional, and I find a lot of vintage workwear just hits that and fits me well. And the story is so compelling to me. The idea of ‘Who’s worn this? What were they getting up to?’ ”

“What drew me to the campaign and to the clothes is that there’s a kind of timelessness to that style. My mom thought I looked amazing in the photos. My girlfriend too. My grandma and my sisters thought I looked great. It’s this very accessible and sort of classically cool thing they have going on. And I felt like me. I felt confident—so much so that I stole several items from the set.”

“With shows and movies, the fear is that it’s a one-of-one item that you might need back for another season. But that’s when you hit them with the ‘pretty, pretty please?’ You say, ‘I swear to God, I’ll guard it with my life.’ And they say, ‘Okay, but you can’t tell anybody.’ With a campaign, it’s probably easier because they’re excited for you to have it and represent. But probably 80 percent of my closet is basically just clothes stolen from characters and sets. It’s fun because you get to try on a bunch of versions of yourself and learn what you like.

“For instance, with Jimmy Olsen in the first Superman movie, they did a fantastic job with this retro-future thing. And then in The People v. Gorilla Grodd, they went a little more vintage-y in a way that felt more like me, Skyler, personally. I tried to steal almost the entire closet.”

“Well, right now I’m doing press for Primetime and Focker-in-Law. I’m doing another movie for A24 called Goblin that I’m not sure how much I can say about; I really don’t want to get fired. But I’m so excited. It’s the most insane script I think I have ever read, and it’s definitely a big swing. We have Man of Tomorrow coming out in the summer, and then Shrek 5. The number of texts I got when I got Shrek … ‘You made it, man! Oh my God! I had no idea you made it!’ I was like, ‘Thank you. Yeah, baby! Shrek 5!’

“But ultimately, it’s a very exciting moment because it’s the first time I have people asking, ‘What do you want to do?’ And I’m like, ‘Damn! I never really thought about that!’ I feel like Primetime lit a fire under me in a real way. And even though I’ve kind of been doing this my whole life, I feel a sense of drive that I’ve never felt really in this way before. So, yeah, man, I’m excited.”

#6815. 4 Epic New Watches Every Watch Guy Is Salivating Over Right Now

Welcome to Watch Guy Watches, GQ’s monthly curation of high-end timepieces for the true watch nerds among us. This month, Moser goes retro(grade); Lange brings back a beloved bracelet; Biver drops its first hand-wound three-hander; and Breitling puts a pair of vintage-inspired watches in its core catalog.

There are only a few firm absolutes in the horological sphere. One of them is “never bet against H. Moser & Cie.” The high-end independent maker never ceases to amaze with its creativity, wit, and technological prowess. From a mechanical timepiece that bears a suspicious resemblance to a certain famous smartwatch to one the coolest integrated bracelet-bearing luxury sports watches on the market, Moser consistently delivers the Swiss-made goods.

Building upon its Pioneer Retrograde Seconds Midnight Blue release from two years ago, three new models with retrograde seconds join the collection this month in the Pioneer, Endeavour, and Streamliner collections. While all three are beautiful, however, it’s the latter whose aesthetics pair particularly well with this steampunk-esque complication—one in which a small hand tracks the passing seconds in an arc and then jumps back to its starting position at the top of each 30-second period.

Housed in a 40mm brushed stainless steel cushion case with a serpentine integrated bracelet, the Streamliner Retrograde Seconds offers a potent mix of Streamline Moderne and futurism before one even considers its dial. Executed in midnight blue fumé enamel, it features applied indices and the brand’s signature modified syringe handset, with a faint “H. Moser & Cie” signature appearing in white beneath 12 o’clock. The lower half of the face, meanwhile, is given over to the retrograde seconds display. Inspired by instrument gauges such as automotive speedometers, it features smooth action powered by the cal. HMC 252, a product of movement specialists Agenhor. (Flip the watch over and you can view its tungsten rotor whirring away via a sapphire caseback.)

One of the coolest features of the Streamliner collection is that despite the presence of numerous complications; of the use of fumé dials; and of high-end pricing, these watches are meant to be used. With 120m of water resistance, steel constriction, automatic winding, and 72 hours of power reserve, the Streamliner Retrograde Seconds is effectively a high-end tool watch. (Though at a sticker price of 29,900 CHF, maybe take it off if you’re, like, climbing Mt. Everest or whatever?)

While retrograde seconds displays don’t have the same utility as calendar readouts or GMT complications, they do demonstrate horological competence on the part of the watchmaker…and more importantly, they’re wild fun to watch in action. And if you can’t have a little fun in your watch-collecting journey, well—then what the heck are you even doing here, anyway?!

If you’ve been waiting years for the return of platinum bracelet options for A. Lange & Söhne’s Datograph Perpetual watch, we give you permission to rejoice: a new version of the timepiece—made in honor of the model’s 20th anniversary—is equipped not only with a diamond-studded black dial, but with a fresh matching platinum bracelet. Designed with five links rather than the seven of early-2000s models made by German jewelry manufacturer Wellendorff, said bracelet is perhaps more notable than the watch itself, which features the brand’s manually wound manufacture Calibre L952.1 movement and is limited to just 50 pieces.

When watch-world elder statesman Jean-Cladue Biver and his son Pierre co-founded their eponymous firm in 2022, their introductory product was a half-million-dollar automatic tourbillon and minute repeater. Having since added a simple automatic three-hander to their lineup in the form of the Automatique, they’re now expanding their offerings with a hand-wound three-hander in stainless steel. Dubbed the AB, it measures 38mm wide and 9.8mm tall and features a beautiful black multi-level onyx dial with gold accents, plus a cool stainless steel bracelet with an angular five-link design and a patented micro-adjust system. Powered by the new Calibre JCB-004 designed in collaboration with Le Cercle des Horlogers, it’s the brand’s entry-level model…though it’ll still set you back roughly the cost of a BMW M3. (Naturally, it’s already sold out.)

On the slightly less eye-watering—but no less compelling—end of the watch-guy spectrum is the new Breitling Navitimer B09 Chronograph 41. Why does it look so familiar, you might ask? Effectively, it’s a modern version of the famed ref. 806 “Twin Jet”-logo chronograph from the 1960s, only this time, it’s available as a non-limited edition—a fact that will surely excite deep Breitling aficionados. Measuring 41mm in stainless steel, it’s manually wound just like its vintage namesake, and comes paired to a brown leather strap for $7,900 or to a handsome steel bracelet for $8,400. If you fancy something a bit more left-field, there’s also a hand-wound, non-limited version of the Cosmonaute, a riff on the Navitimer equipped with a 24-hour dial that was famously worn by astronaut Scott Carpenter in 1962 ($10,600-$11,000).

#6811. 7 Celebrity Outfits You Should Steal This Month

With the holidays looming and next year’s awards season on the horizon, there’s no rest for the A-list weary. Between hitting up screenings, press junkets, and the general business of being a star, you’ll occasionally catch famous folks kicking their feet up in suites at SoFi Stadium or risking a courtside collision with a referee at an NBA game.

From Harry Styles getting his heart rate up on morning runs over the Williamsburg Bridge to Ethan Hawke turning up for a SAG screening, Glenn Close pulling an absolute grail piece from a film she starred in alongside Robert Redford, and Nicholas Hoult hopping between planes, the celebrity style circuit is showing no signs of slowing down.

Maybe this is the season you’ve been influenced to go all in on corduroy, resurrect those vintage graphic tees gathering dust in your tote bins, or finally dip your dainty toes into the jazz shoe craze. Whatever your sartorial itch, let these celebrities do the legwork for you.

The Hawkester has worn corduroy before, and this look calls to mind Robin Williams in Dead Poets Society, whose affinity for the fabric felt like part of the curriculum. Hawke styles the suit in a way that feels less adjunct professor and more stoner student, thanks to a ratty graphic tee and tinted shades. Rather than dress shoes, he opts for well-worn dark brown Sambas, which contrast nicely with his acorn-colored suit. Carpe diem, and all that.

The star of Netflix’s upcoming show The Altruists demonstrates how dressing in a flowy, monochromatic palette doesn’t have to feel boring. The burgundy trench coat injects just enough color to keep things interesting. Leave it open so you don’t look like Inspector Gadget, and throw on tinted sunglasses, because they’re never a bad idea, no matter the fit.

With his Together, Together tour wrapping up at the end of the month, Harry’s New York stroll fits will likely be inducted into the annals of celeb big fits. He seems to have tapped into the lore of ’90s-era skaters, layering a long-sleeved tee under a T-shirt. Add light-wash jeans, all-white sneakers, and Apple earbuds, and you’ve got a man who feels gloriously analog in a digital world.

Glenn Close shows us how to build an outfit around a statement piece, in this case, a jacket from The Natural, the 1984 film in which she co-starred. Throw on navy slacks, loafers, and a crisp white Oxford shirt, and you’re good to go. Of course, it helps when you have iconic films attached to your name, too.

Example 1,088 of why denim-on-denim is always the answer to the in-flight outfit dilemma. Mr. Hoult opts for a charcoal-hued Canadian tuxedo, offset by black boots and a dad cap. Denim can take a beating. Whether from turbulence-induced coffee spills or an overenthusiastic recline, it provides a welcome layer of warmth on those cold and bone-dry flights.

As the oldest brother in Sweden’s Skarsgård acting dynasty, Alexander proves that an age-old graphic tee and Chuck Taylors will always be a can’t-miss combination. The louche, open trench coat adds a bit of flare to the fit. We also have to note, when you’re 6’4” with a chin chiseled enough to double as an ice luge, it’s hard for anything to look bad on you.

People love to claim that black and brown don’t belong together. I guess they’ve never had a Turtle Pecan Blizzard. The black shirt and blazer flirt with Y2K corporate-core, but the khaki-colored, pleated, baggy chinos and jazz shoes give the whole kit some much-needed attitude.
