#6809. The 4 Best Super Automatic Espresso Machines

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

These robot baristas will do every little step for you—except latte art.

Here’s something I think someone out there needs to hear: Not everything should be easy. Not every problem should be outsourced to the robots. Oh, ChatGPT can make a podcast about your kids’ days. That’s a shortcut that leads you nowhere. One thing, however, can be totally handed over to the robots with no consequence. At-home coffee. You can make espresso a hobby, turn it into rocket science—some on our Esquire staff do just that. But most of us just want our morning latte or Americano, a caffeine fix to get us going. If that’s you, the best gift you can get yourself is a fully automatic espresso machine—or, as it’s known by its more fun name, a super automatic espresso machine.

I love my Breville grinder (it makes the task quick work every time), but some folks just want simplicity and a latte on demand. Can’t say I blame them. An onboard hopper and grinder is one of the boxes a super automatic espresso machine must check. The others? A digital screen that lets you select beverages, adjustable settings and user profiles, and, most importantly, it must do all the weighing and measuring for you, ensuring it brews the same drink every time. Many, but not all, come with milk frothers and tanks to hold your milk or milk alternative.

At their best, the process is as easy as filling it up with beans and milk and then letting the warm-up rinse cycle boot. After that, any hot coffee or espresso drink you want (and sometimes cold-brewed ones too) is available at the push of a button. Some add extra steps, but the core of it is no measuring, no tamping, no wasted time. And not a single wasted cup. From De’Longhi, KitchenAid, and Terra Kaffe, these are the best super automatic espresso machines at every price point that will brew you a consistent espresso every time.

If you’ve read about products on Esquire for any amount of time, you know that we stan De’Longhi. My editor Luke Guillory and I can attest: When we see Magnifica, we say “magnificent.” For the price, the Magnifica Duo is both an excellent value and also everything you really need in an automatic coffee machine. It comes with a milk tank equipped with a foaming wand and dispenser. That whole apparatus is completely dishwasher safe so you don’t have to worry about scrubbing a thing. You are buying an automatic espresso machine after all.

It doesn’t have an LCD touch screen, but the touch-sensitive buttons pair with a small screen to make drink selection take a matter of seconds. It can be a bit clunky at first, but getting used to it doesn’t take much time. From there, you can program quick selects to get straight to your favorite drinks in even less time. The machine cleans itself and, eventually, it will pay for itself too.

For me, the most important thing an espresso machine can automate is maintenance. I’m always forgetting to clean my coffee makers (among the dozens of other gadgets I need to keep clean), but I never have to worry about that with my KitchenAid. This super automatic espresso machine and iced coffee maker rinses the machine and milk tube routinely, and in the settings I can always see how close a part is to needing a cleaning. On top of that, it’s a super easy machine that makes tasty beverages. It doesn’t have the 50 plus drinks of a $2,000 De’Longhi, but you have most of your basics including cappuccinos, lattes, and even iced coffee and iced espresso.

The KF3 is the least expensive of KitchenAid’s line of super automatics because it doesn’t come with a milk tank. Instead, the AutoMilk system it does have is brilliant. Just stick the provided milk tube into any vessel and it will extract the proper amount of liquid into the dispenser tank above your cup, where it is steamed and frothed to the ideal temp. After your drink is ready, you’ll be prompted (but not forced!) to run a rinse of the milk tube. I recommend it; keep that stuff clean.

And finally, it’s worth mentioning this machine is made from PFAS-free parts. This means a much reduced risk to exposure to microplastics in your coffee. Everyone can appreciate that.

If having something that looks great on your counter while it does the bean grinding and shot pulling for you is the priority, you have to take a look at Terra Kaffe’s machines. The sleek, minimalist design says “retro future” without the rest of your kitchen needing to fit any specific vibe. You use a stainless steel dial to literally “dial in” your brew strength and temperature. It’s the perfect machine for those who drink a daily espresso or Americano.

I mention those drinks because, as any coffee drinker will know, they don’t contain milk. While it’s coming in at a nice price, the Demi does admittedly lack a milk foaming option. If you already own a milk frother at home, though, this might be a way to save some money and still get a fantastic, no-work espresso machine.

Here’s the tea. This is an older model—2023 is ancient by gadget standards—and they are getting more difficult to find in stock. At least this means the price has gone down... a little bit. The fact remains, every one of the coffee freaks on Reddit swears by the Eletta Explore. It is still the pinnacle of what De’Longhi’s super automatic machines can do, with dozens and dozens of hot and cold drink recipes at your very fingertips. The milk frothing tech is top notch and pretty much all of the parts are dishwasher safe for cleaning. It even includes a travel mug for those mornings when time isn’t on your side.

The only complaint I can see raised against it is that, at this price, many competitors have swappable bean hoppers. The $1,300 De’Longhi Rivelia has a bean swap system, where you can switch between two different bean hoppers—decaf drinkers love this for switching over in the afternoon. Users report mixed results since you still have to clear an unseeable number of beans from the grinder before the switch is complete. This can land you in a no-man’s-land where you might be drinking half-caf on accident. It’s why I’m still recommending the Eletta over that machine.

#6804. Beyoncé, Blue Ivy, and Tina Knowles Show Off Their Individual Styles in Rare Three-Generation Photo

The trio attended Jay-Z’s final show at SoFi Stadium

A little over a week ago, Jay-Z held the final shows of his Jay-Z 30 Tour at SoFi Stadium in Inglewood, California, where he had the support of his wife, Beyoncé, at both back-to-back concerts.

But Beyoncé wasn’t the only loved one in the cheering section. As seen in a series of photos shared by the “Texas Hold ’Em” singer’s stylist, Ty Hunter, other starry attendees included former Destiny’s Child members Kelly Rowland and Michelle Williams, as well as Beyoncé and Jay’s eldest daughter, Blue Ivy. Even Queen Bey’s mom, Tina Knowles, came out to watch.

On Jay-Z’s second night at SoFi, Beyoncé posed for a rare three-generation photo with her mom and eldest child, where each woman showed off her individual style (as seen in slide four above).

For her part, the pop superstar wore a bone-colored set from the LaPointe Spring/Summer 2027 ready-to-wear collection, which consisted of a sheer wrap top layered underneath a cropped jacket and paired with matching boyshorts, both of which were decorated with silver gemstones.

As for Blue Ivy, she wore a striped baseball top tucked into cutoff jean shorts, and she accessorized with Timberland boots, frameless sunglasses, and a black bandana nestled into her pocket.

Meanwhile, mama Knowles chose a tartan off-the-shoulder blouse with black leather pants and inky pointed heels. Talk about three generations of stylish women.

The last time we saw this trio together was back in October, when Beyoncé celebrated the opening of the new Knowles-Rowland House in Houston, Texas. At the unveiling, the Grammy winner was joined onstage by her mom, and they eventually called up Blue Ivy and Beyoncé’s other daughter, Rumi, who both helped to cut the ceremonial ribbon.

#6802. Can the Brooklyn Nets Convert a Generation of Newly Minted Knicks Fans?

At the Brooklyn Nets’ home opener against the Charlotte Hornets last week, one longtime fan pointed out the strange dynamic inherent to New York City basketball fandom. He was jealous of places like Boston and Philadelphia, he said, because the entire city organizes itself around one team. Sports franchises, after all, are avatars of civic pride, and the very best organizations come to reflect the values of the cities they represent. (Think the Showtime Lakers or the Grit-and-Grind Memphis Grizzlies.) But in New York, home to both the Nets and the Knicks, fan bases are pitted against each other by fans, media, and sometimes even franchise leaders.

For a long time, the Nets could take solace in their cross rivals' decades-long stretch of ineptitude. That vanished when Jalen Brunson and company lifted the Larry O’Brien in June, leaving Brooklyn with some soul searching to do. “It doesn't make us feel very good,” Nets owner Joe Tsai told NetsDaily over the summer. “It irates our fan base and it is embarrassing.” As The Ringer’s Zach Lowe pointed out on his recently launched Substack, the Knicks’ title cost the Nets “an entire generation of fans.”

I, for one, have never understood why commentators are so obsessed with the size of Brooklyn’s fanbase. I am, obviously, a hugely biased homer, but you don’t hear the same concerns when it comes to other little-brother teams like the Clippers or teams, like Wizards, whose games are sparsely attended. Even as they’ve tanked these past couple of years, the Nets have maintained a 99% attendance rate and are the eighth most-valuable franchise in the entire league. Still, I have to hear talking heads argue that the team should move back to New Jersey? I might be salty, but that’s stupid.

Nevertheless, there is clearly angst about building up a coalition of Nets faithful. On his podcast Curious Mike, where I wish he would be less curious, the team’s star forward Michael Porter Jr. recently said, “When we play Knicks, Celtics, Philly, Lakers, it's mostly their fans. It's crazy.” And he’s not wrong: it's a weird time to be a fan of the Brooklyn Nets—the team’s fallen twice in the lottery and is trying to kickstart a rebuild in the shadow of one of the most storied franchises in the NBA. So I made my way over to the team’s training facility in Sunset Park to see how the Nets are tackling this problem with pillars of the present and future.

“I don't give a shit,” says Julius Randle, tucked into a glass room in the corner of the Nets’ famously photogenic training facility, when I ask him about the invasion of opposing fans at Barclays. “I've been on teams that weren't good and didn’t have the excitement around them. Then you start winning and the whole arena is there to see your team and cheer. So I think that's just part of being a fan. For us as a team, we got to give them something to root for.”

Randle, who played an integral role in the Knicks rebuild before being shipped off to the Minnesota Timberwolves in 2024, has seen New York City basketball fandom from both sides of the river. “It doesn't really matter what team I play for. I was at my son's basketball game yesterday and it was like, Man, I'm a Nets fan. You’re going to kill it this year. Then I get on the other side and it's, Thank you for everything you've done for the Knicks."

Randle, now on his fifth team in 13 seasons, also downplayed the idea that the Nets-Knicks intercity rivalry mattered all that much to players. “From the locker room, basketball is basketball,” he insists. “It's not like college or high school where we play our rival on Friday or whatever. You play 82 games a year. You feel the energy during the game, but I definitely think it's a bigger deal from a fan perspective than it is from a player's perspective.”

I can’t even finish my next question before he cuts me off. Do you see part of your role as growing this next generation of fans or are you just here to—

“No, hell no,” Randle says. “I definitely want to be a part of establishing a culture and giving the city of Brooklyn something to be proud of. I'm not here just to come out and get my numbers or whatever it is. I'm here to win a championship. I'm here to help these young guys excel and speed up their growth process.”

One of those young guys is the anointed prince of the franchise, Mikel Brown Jr., the Orlando-born point guard the Nets took sixth overall in this year’s draft and who fans are already calling “the best prospect in franchise history” (I agree.) Another prominent member of Nets Twitter, moved by preseason action, described him as a mix of CJ McCollum and All-NBA regulars Tyrese Haliburton and Kyrie Irving. (I love that tweet, and I love this fanbase.)

With the Nets, Brown came into a peculiar situation. Not only does the future of the franchise’s success hinge on whether he can reach his star potential—so does the team’s ability to cultivate a new generation of fans. It’s a challenge Brown recognizes and embraces. Many fans, myself included, took note of a phrase he kept repeating on draft night: “I’m home.” It felt like a full-throated endorsement of the franchise and its mission. “Most of the [Nets] fans are there in the arena for a draft night,” Brown says when I ask him what he meant. “So to hear the crowd pop and just to see the energy, it brought me a whole bunch of joy.”

Brown is 20 years old, and looks even younger, but he understands just how much is riding on his rookie season. “I embrace it,” he says. “At the end of the day, we're just kind of focusing on what we can control and then let everything else play on. Whatever happens after that and whoever comes along with us, enjoy the ride because we're going to go to war.”

Everyone I talked to believes Brown has the goods to deliver on both his promises and his potential. Randle, too, told me he’s been texting his friends back home to tell them how gifted Brown is. “I've been thoroughly, thoroughly impressed with not just his talent level, but his maturity and his IQ at such a young age is really, really impressive.” Typically, Randle continues, young players are single-mindedly focused on scoring. But Brown understands that there’s more than one way to contribute. “He just knows how to make the right play every single time. He just knows how to play good basketball.”

Brown is the type of prospect us Nets fans have been waiting to call our own. I loved rooting for every iteration of this team, but what the franchise’s best players—Jason Kidd, Deron Williams, Joe Johnson, Kyrie Irving, and Kevin Durant—have in common is that none of them were drafted by the Nets. Developing a homegrown star is a different task altogether, and the Nets have solid reason to believe they've found one.

On Wednesday night against the Boston Celtics, we caught a glimpse of what Brown can do . The 20 year-old was “not human,” as Nets longtime commentator Ian Eagle likes to say, in the fourth quarter. After pouring in several clutch baskets, he sent the game to overtime with a falling-to-the-side three with just 0.2 seconds left. Brooklyn lost the game, but if Brown’s development keeps trending in this direction, maybe we can win the war.

My Nets fandom is the result of perfect timing. I mostly grew up in California but spent my formative sports-team-choosing years in New Jersey when my family and I moved across the country for my dad’s job. After moving back to the Bay Area halfway through elementary school, I clung even more tightly to the Nets to stay connected with the friends and the place I left behind: the Snoopy fishing rod I used at the local lake, the fireflies in my New Jersey-sized backyard, the cul-de-sac that seemed to have a friend in every neighboring house.

During the Nets home opener, with the team wearing their excellent ABA-inspired red-and-blue jerseys, the game seemed all but decided as the Charlotte Hornets started to pull away at the end of the fourth. But with just over a minute left, the crowd started to pop as Randle bullied his way into an and-one layup and starting center Day'Ron Sharpe blocked a shot at the other end.

I thought back to the simple philosophy both Randle and Brown echoed: just win, and people will show up. The Nets eventually lost that game against Charlotte, and a few other heartbreakers since (against very tough competition, for what it’s worth). It’s unclear whether the Nets will ever convert a generation of fans spellbound by the Knicks, or even win the Finals. But I know I’ll be there.

#6800. Can Money Buy You A Better Brain?

We’ve spent years optimizing everything from our glucose levels and gut microbiomes to our sleep scores and biological ages. Full-body MRIs, smart rings and an arsenal of light therapies have turned our bodies into wired-up machines for monitoring, measuring and fine-tuning. But what about the organ responsible for making sense of it all? As anxieties mount over how a life spent online — and increasingly outsourced to AI — is eroding our ability to think, wellness has a new obsession: the brain.

As with so many wellness tech trends, the Kardashians were ahead of the curve. In a 2022 episode of The Kardashians, Kendall Jenner took Khloé to Amen Clinics for a SPECT scan, a type of imaging that measures blood flow in the brain. Her results were interpreted as revealing anxiety and the effects of past emotional trauma. At the time, it seemed over the top, a scientifically contentious form of indulgent self-examination, most fitting for people with money to burn.

Now, brain health is moving beyond celebrity curiosity and into luxury wellness, from neurofeedback sessions at Mandarin Oriental Mayfair to Clinique La Prairie’s seven-day cognitive longevity program in Switzerland, costing just under CHF 30,000 ($36,000). Meanwhile, neurotechnology companies are bringing brain monitoring and stimulation into specialist clinics and at-home devices, hoping to make cognitive fitness as routine as going to the gym.

Consumers appear hungry to know more. According to McKinsey’s 2025 Future of Wellness report, 61% of respondents consider cognitive function very important, yet brain health remains one of the areas where consumer demand is going unmet. Younger consumers, especially Gen Z, reportedly feel that gap the most.

“For a long time, it was all about physical performance, having beautiful top-performing bodies as late as possible in life,” says Olga Donica, longevity innovation director at luxury longevity clinic Clinique La Prairie. “Now, we are switching to something much more advanced, complex and sophisticated, and this for sure is the brain.”

Part of the appeal lies in growing research into neuroplasticity, which has overturned the idea that the brain becomes fixed in adulthood, revealing its capacity to rewire itself and adapt throughout life. For wellness brands, it’s an enticing proposition: if they’ve built lucrative businesses helping us optimize our bodies to perform better for longer, why not our brains?

In June, Mandarin Oriental Mayfair added a Brain Gym residency to its wellness offering, led by Natalia Ramsden, performance psychologist and founder of Sofos Associates, a cognitive performance consultancy. The experience begins with a 75 to 90-minute brain-mapping assessment, during which clients wear a cap fitted with 24 sensors that record electrical activity as they rest and complete short cognitive tasks. The results inform personalized recommendations and, for those who want to continue, a course of 10 to 20 neurofeedback sessions, where an on-screen game responds to their brain activity in real time. Some clients return for maintenance sessions and repeat assessments. “You don’t get fit in one session at the gym, and the brain is no different,” Ramsden says.

It’s a far cry from a doctor’s office. Sessions take place in the hotel’s private residences and suites, though most bookings come from external clients rather than hotel guests — largely business leaders, founders, and entrepreneurs based around Mayfair.

“My work is with leaders, founders, and investors who are already performing well and want to keep doing so under sustained pressure,” she says. “Many come to us running on high alert. They can’t switch off, their sleep suffers, and under pressure their attention narrows when they most need to see the bigger picture.”

Ramsden calls this investment in mental performance “cognitive capital”. With longer working lives and growing dependence on technology fueling anxieties about mental sharpness, Ramsden says, her clients are getting younger, too. “We’ve spent years talking about lifespan and healthspan. Now, people are starting to talk about brainspan,” she adds. For luxury hotels, this could mean a new frontier in wellness, offering a way to build lasting relationships with affluent clients beyond the occasional stay.

Where Sofos caters to professionals hoping to maintain their mental edge, Clinique La Prairie addresses a related anxiety: what happens when that edge begins to dull with age?

Clinique La Prairie in Switzerland, where clients can undergo a seven-day ‘Brain Potential’ program focused on cognitive longevity. Photos: Courtesy of Clinique La Prairie

Donica says the retreat’s Brain Potential program attracts predominantly affluent professionals in their late 40s to early 60s, often at the height of their careers but beginning to notice changes in their cognitive performance. They report problems with memory, stress, recovery and decision-making. “They run big companies, they own big things. So they need to be cognitively sharp,” Donica says.

Developed over two years, in collaboration with Lausanne University Hospital, the seven-day program at the clinic’s Montreux property combines brain MRI and volumetric analysis with cognitive testing, genetic screening, inflammation markers, gut microbiome analysis, and cardiovascular examinations. Guests receive a calculated “cognitive age”, with the results used to devise personalized nutrition, exercise and cognitive training plans. Each has a dedicated team of five: a doctor, nutritionist, therapist, movement expert, and guest-relations specialist. Guests leave with a personalized, three-month cognitive training plan, while follow-up assessments allow the clinic to adjust its recommendations over time.

But the ability to measure more aspects of brain health does not necessarily mean the ability to improve them. Donica says the clinic is gathering observations from guests, while acknowledging that these are not sufficient to make scientific claims about the program’s effectiveness. It’s a revealing tension: the higher the price and the more sophisticated the diagnostics, the more compelling the promise of personalization — even as evidence of lasting cognitive benefits remains limited.

At the more accessible end of the market, businesses are looking to turn brain health from an occasional luxury splurge into a regular part of consumers’ wellness routines.

London-based Naya Health, founded in 2025 by Daniel and Emily Honeywell, is among a new wave of clinics bringing brain stimulation into the wellness market. It offers repetitive transcranial magnetic stimulation (rTMS), a non-invasive treatment that uses magnetic pulses to stimulate targeted brain regions, for clinical conditions and now also marketed toward cognitive optimization. The co-founders are bullish on the technology’s potential, touting benefits from alleviating depression, anxiety and chronic fatigue to sharpening focus, improving decision-making and boosting athletic performance.

Honeywell likens brain stimulation to physiotherapy — “a workout for the brain” — that needs to be tailored to a person’s condition, rather than administered at a fixed intensity. Rather than treating stimulation as a one-off intervention, the clinic encourages clients to return for maintenance sessions, with some executives and athletes also booking “top-up” appointments ahead of important meetings or competitions.

Currently, around 80% of Naya’s clients are seeking treatment for chronic conditions, while 20% come for optimization. Optimization sessions cost around $300, while sessions for chronic conditions cost approximately $470. Some longer treatment courses can amount to around $10,700.

A patient receives an rTMS treatment at Naya Health’s London clinic. Photo: Courtesy of Naya Health

“The traditional model is transactional: people feel sick, then they receive treatment,” Daniel says. “People increasingly want long-term prevention and proactive care that keeps them well, in the same way they go to the gym to maintain their physical health. In five years, asking someone about their brain health routine will be as normal as asking about their skincare.”

And it’s not just brain stimulation attracting investment. Neurotechnology company Myndspan is building a business around measuring the brain rather than treating it, offering £600 assessments using magnetoencephalography (MEG), a technology traditionally confined to hospitals and research laboratories. The scans record magnetic signals generated by neural activity, giving customers a snapshot of their brain function and an estimated cognitive age.

Co-founder and CEO Caitlin Baltzer says her longer-term ambition is to make brain scans a recurring health check rather than a specialist medical procedure. “The value today is the information it provides and the ability to build a personal history,” Baltzer says. “Demonstrating the longer-term benefit is the work ahead for us.”

Beyond luxury hospitality and specialist clinics, dozens of neurotechnology companies are bringing brain monitoring and stimulation into everyday wearables.

Muse, the EEG (electroencephalogram, a technology that measures the brain’s electrical activity) headband maker, launched Brain Age earlier this month — a feature reminiscent of Whoop’s most popular feature, the Whoop Age — that estimates whether Muse’s 500,000 users’ brain activity appears younger or older than their biological age. Ongoing weekly assessments require a premium subscription, creating another potential revenue stream. While consumer EEG sacrifices some signal strength compared with laboratory equipment, CMO Nadia Kumentas argues that repeated measurements offer a different advantage. “In return, you get data from real life, night after night,” she says.

Muse is a consumer-grade EEG headband that claims to help users improve focus, relaxation, and meditation through neurofeedback that monitors brainwave activity in real time. Photo: Courtesy of Muse

Neurotechnology company Neurable has embedded EEG sensors into $499 noise-canceling headphones to monitor focus and cognitive fatigue, but founder and CEO Ramses Alcaide sees greater potential in licensing the technology to other manufacturers — including mainstream wearables brands. “We believe brain sensing will eventually become a standard feature in wearables, much like heart rate and sleep tracking today,” he says.

Others are moving beyond measurement to intervention. Pulsetto, which produces neck-worn vagus nerve stimulation devices, saw revenue rise from approximately $9 million in 2024 to more than $40 million in 2025, according to co-founder and CEO Povilas Sabaliauskas. “Data on its own doesn’t change how you feel, and now, consumers need a practical way to act on all their health information,” Sabaliauskas says.

It mirrors the broader trend in the longevity tech and wellness market — after years of selling consumers information about their bodies, they’re increasingly selling interventions to change how they function. As the technology becomes more portable, and the price barrier is lowered, there are more opportunities for consumer wellness brands, as well as luxury hospitality and spas, to sell the promise of a sharper brain.

But experts warn that identifying a market is one thing; proving these products actually work is another, particularly when sophisticated neuroscience-adjacent therapeutic applications are supposedly re-packaged in consumer-grade devices for at-home use.

Isaac Treves, a postdoctoral research fellow at Columbia University who studies neuroimaging, mindfulness and resilience, is particularly skeptical of consumer neurofeedback devices such as Muse. Beyond the challenges of capturing reliable EEG signals outside a laboratory, he questions whether those signals can tell us much about more subjective, complex mental states. “We don’t actually know how to predict whether you are paying attention or meditating peacefully with brain signals,” he says, arguing that “the Muse algorithm is mostly guesswork”.

“Unfortunately, the evidence base for neurofeedback devices like Muse is extremely limited, and most likely contaminated by placebo effects,” he adds.

None of this means brain health is destined to become another discarded wellness fad. Concerns about cognitive aging are real, and Treves remains optimistic about the potential of technologies that measure and influence brain activity.

But the industry’s commercial momentum is running ahead of the evidence for some of its most ambitious claims. Treves believes increasingly personalized neuroscience, supported by larger datasets and AI, could eventually produce meaningful advances. For now, though, his advice for improving cognitive wellbeing involves no re-wiring of the brain. “Good sleep and exercise habits, good social connection, seeking out meaning in your life, and all those obvious points that our parents tell us,” he advises.

Pulsetto’s vagus nerve stimulator is one of an emerging category of at-home consumer devices promising to regulate the nervous system and brain. Photo: Courtesy of Pulsetto

For luxury wellness and hospitality brands, the stakes are reputational. When selling a better brain, scientific credibility matters more than hyperpersonalization or luxurious surroundings.

There’s also a more uncomfortable question: whether the pursuit of brain ‘optimization’ risks becoming another demand on our already overstretched minds. “There’s a general anxiety about doing the latest thing, being the best, and capturing and quantifying everything,” says Sofos founder Ramsden. “Tracking can be very useful, but it can also backfire.”

Do Smart Glasses Have a Surveillance Problem?

What Fashion Tech Leaders Are Reading This Summer

#6798. Better American Sunscreens Are Here—But We’re Still Way Behind the Best

Anyone who’s applied sunscreen only to be left with a chalky white cast, a greasy finish knows American sun care has long been a frustrating experience. The US is decades behind European and Asian counterparts, which have long been blessed with sunscreens that are lighter, more luxurious, and easier to wear every day. For skincare obsessives (this writer included) trips abroad are also opportunities to stock up on superior sunscreens they can’t find at home.

The root of the problem is regulation. The Food and Drug Administration classifies sunscreen as a drug rather than a cosmetic. Because of that distinction, more time and testing is required to get new sunscreens on the shelves, dramatically reducing innovation in American sun protection. But for the first time since the ‘90s, we’ve had some movement on that front. In June of this year, the FDA approved a new sunscreen filter called bemotrizinol—also known as Tinosorb S or BEMT—after years of lobbying in the states by dermatologists, skincare brands, and Congress members. It’s a meaningful step toward better sun care, but the US is still lagging behind.

Bemotrizinol is prized for its ability to provide both UVA and UVB protection while remaining stable in sunlight. That’s a significant advantage over some existing US-approved filters, which offer protection against one type of UV radiation more effectively than the other. BEMT also has the potential to make sunscreen more pleasant to wear: “And because it’s a large, oil-loving molecule, it mostly stays on the surface of your skin instead of soaking in, and it blends into lightweight, non-greasy formulas with no white cast,” says Dr. Joel Spitz, co-founder, chief medical officer, and board-certified dermatologist at Honeydew, a virtual dermatology platform.

Bemotrizinol might be new for the US market, but it’s already been used in sunscreen formulations abroad for 20 years, Spitz says. That long track record helps explain why consumers in the US have spent years coveting the more cosmetically elegant formulas available in countries like South Korea and France. While most consumers would love if major brands like Neutrogena, La Roche-Posay, or Beauty of Joseon brought their European or Asian formulations to the US, those SPFs still contain filters that the FDA hasn’t approved. As a result, many brands are developing entirely new BEMT-centric formulas for use stateside.

Australian label Ultra Violette was the first brand to release a BEMT sunscreen in the US with its Unblock Screen SPF 50 in August 2026. Neutrogena debuted its Ultra Sheer Daily Face Sunscreen SPF 70 in September. Earlier this month French drugstore staple La Roche-Posay put its Anthelios UVDefense Sunscreen SPF 50 up for sale online. Don’t mistake the relatively quick arrival of these products after the FDA’s approval for ease in which brands can develop new formulations. According to Emanuella Louis, La Roche-Posay’s public relations manager, the brand had been working on its new formula for more than two years.

That long lead time reflects the way sunscreen development works: Brands can research and test formulas in anticipation of regulatory changes, then move a product forward when a particular filter gets the green light. Louis says La Roche-Posay has access to filters the FDA may be considering for approval and is “actively working, playing, testing on what possibly those formulas would be.” When a filter is approved, she explains, a formula already in the testing pipeline can be moved up.

Some brands are still tinkering with BEMT formulas for the US. Round Lab, one of South Korea’s most popular sunscreen manufacturers, has sold a version of its SPF in the US since 2024, and is hard at work on a new version of those formulas that contains bemotrizinol.

“Bemotrizinol’s approval in the US is a meaningful change, but it does not mean a Korean formula can simply be brought to the US market as is,” says Koochul Kwon, Round Lab’s chief technology officer. Beyond incorporating the newly approved filter, brands must ensure the ingredients keep the UV filters stably dissolved and working alongside other components, including moisturizing ingredients. The finished product must then meet US requirements for SPF and broad-spectrum testing, product stability, and labeling. With those considerations in mind, Kwon expects Round Lab to release its own bemotrizinol sunscreen sometime in 2027.

A sunscreen is only as useful as a person’s willingness to wear it consistently. Bemotrizinol’s ability to protect against UV damage is important, but its potential to make formulas lighter, less greasy, and more comfortable could be just as consequential to get people to implement it in their everyday skincare routines.

“One of the strengths of Korean sun care is how naturally it has built sun protection into the everyday skincare experience,” Kwon says. “For people to apply enough sunscreen and reapply it when needed, the way it feels matters as much as how well it protects.”

But better filters alone won’t transform American sun care. The real breakthrough will be sunscreens that people actually enjoy wearing—and, as a result, are more likely to apply generously and reapply throughout the day, a key step to ensuring its effectiveness.

Charlotte Palermino, cofounder and chief brand officer of skincare brand Dieux, has been an advocate for easier sunscreen filter approvals ever since she tried her first Japanese sunscreen, Biore UV, a decade ago. She realized sunscreen could stave off skin issues after her dermatologist told her that her hyperpigmentation had been the result of failing to use sun protection. In the years since she has publicly pushed for regulatory reform. She’s hesitant to put an estimated release date on Dieux’s upcoming BEMT-centric sunscreen, simply because the brand is dialed in to ensure its able to deliver a quality, wearable product.

“We’ve gone through this with [Dieux’s] SkinMercy, which is an OTC colloidal oatmeal cream, but for sunscreen, the stability of your sunscreen is so fucking important,” she says. “If my sunscreen separates or if it’s not at that stated SPF 50, if that degrades at all with broad spectrum protection, I would be mortified.”

Palerimo cites brands like Korea’s Beauty of Joseon, which is more popular in the US than in its home country, as evidence that Americans have long been on the hunt for better sunscreen. And though BEMT is a step forward, we still have a way to go. Board-certified dermatologist Jane Yoo has been lobbying Congress to get new sunscreen filters approved for the US and is tracking the FDA’s progress in making those approvals. Currently there are seven other filters—bisoctrizole, drometrizole trisiloxane, octyl triazone, amiloxate, diethylhexyl butamido triazone iscotrizinol, ecamsule, and enzacamene—under the agency’s Time and Extent Application (TEA) pipeline. Those filters are part of the 2014 Sunscreen Innovation Act meant to speed up the process of determining if ingredients are Generally Recognized as Safe and Effective, or GRASE. But here we are 12 years later, and we’re still waiting for their approval.

“A good analogy is we’ve been making sunscreens with both of our hands tied behind our backs,” Palermino says. “We’ve now had one hand released. I would like both hands released.”

#6796. The Vogue Business AI Tracker

The Vogue Business AI Tracker keeps a record of the most important AI developments that will influence our industry and our world, each week. From venture capital investments and startup launches to product drops and regulatory updates, we’ll make sure you never miss a beat when it comes to the AI news that matters.

Why it matters: Prada has launched a ChatGPT plugin allowing users in the US, the UK and Italy to discover products, explore collections and find stores using information sourced directly from the brand. As AI assistants become a gateway to product discovery, the move gives Prada a more authoritative presence in conversations it might otherwise struggle to influence. “Conversational experiences represent a natural evolution of traditional e-commerce,” said Prada Group digital director Laura Campanella. “Through this pilot initiative, we are exploring a new territory for customer engagement.”

The news coincides with the launch of Prada’s Christmas campaign this week, featuring hand-drawn animation by Disney veteran John Pomeroy. These two contrasting moves reflect a broader distinction emerging in luxury: AI for product discovery, and human craftsmanship for consumer-facing brand storytelling.

The news: Anthropic’s IPO filing reveals the huge costs and risks of the AI boom.

Why it matters: Anthropic’s blockbuster IPO filing offers a rare look inside the economics — and extraordinary risks — of the AI industry. As the Claude AI maker prepares for a potential $2 trillion valuation, the Financial Times reports that its revenues exceeded $33 billion in the first nine months of 2026, while its computing costs have fallen dramatically relative to sales, all suggesting a path to profitability. Yet Anthropic has also committed more than $800 billion to computing infrastructure through 2045, much of it supplied by technology giants including Amazon and Google, who are also major investors.

Crucially, the prospectus devotes 92 pages to risks, warning that future AI models could resist shutdown, manipulate information or cause harm too large for insurers to cover. For businesses embedding AI into everything from creative production to customer service, the filing exposes a central contradiction: the technology is becoming commercially indispensable even as its developers acknowledge profound uncertainty over its long-term risks.

The news: Anthropic plans European AI consultancy.

Why it matters: Anthropic is reportedly partnering with private equity firm Hg to launch a European consultancy that’ll place AI engineers and advisers in external businesses to help them integrate Anthropic’s technology, according to the Financial Times. Expected to launch by the end of 2026, hubs are currently planned in London and Berlin, with the Claude developer likely using the venture to accelerate corporate AI adoption — and even capture some of the work traditionally handled by consultancies such as Accenture and the Big Four.

Fashion and luxury businesses, many of which are still grappling with how to deploy AI, could be fertile ground for these services. But the model raises an obvious tension: while companies may welcome direct access to AI expertise, how independent can the advice be when the consultancy is backed by a company with a commercial interest in driving adoption of its own technology?

The news: Anthropic launches Claude Duet AI agent to rival OpenAI and Meta.

Why it matters: Anthropic has released Claude Duet, an AI agent rivaling Meta’s consumer-focused Muse and OpenAI’s more enterprise-oriented Dots; the Duet has both brand and consumer use cases for fashion. It can purchase products on consumers’ behalf using Shopify’s Shop Pay and Stripe Link, while merchants can instruct the agent to set up, design and manage their Shopify stores, including inventory and orders, Anthropic announced in a blog post. Meanwhile, a “richer visual editor” — which allows brands to design and preview their storefronts within the agent’s interface before anything goes live — is “coming soon”.

It was only a matter of time until Anthropic followed Meta and OpenAI in launching a personal AI assistant. Now, the battle is on between these tech companies to capture both consumer and brands’ trust, so that the integration these agents depend on across stores and payment providers is realized. The growing competition here signals a shift towards AI-mediated purchases, potentially reducing direct customer interactions for brands and making product visibility and seamless checkout across AI platforms increasingly important.

The news: Major banks and retailers block AI shopping agents like Meta Muse.

Why it matters: A growing list of US banks and retailers are blocking AI agents like Meta Muse and Instinct from acting on their websites. Amazon and Ebay have both prohibited these models from browsing or purchasing items on their respective marketplaces, while Citigroup, Bank of America, JPMorgan and Charles Schwab have each moved to prevent outside agents from logging into customer accounts and transacting on their behalf.

“We have to make sure we protect our customers in the right way [and] we’re building tools to do that,” JPMorgan CEO Jamie Dimon said on the company’s third-quarter earnings call last week. “In the meantime, we’re not going to allow just any outside agent to come inside the company, because that’s too risky for clients and for JPMorgan.”

While much of the fashion industry runs its direct-to-consumer sites via platform providers like Shopify — which has opened its network to AI agents unless a brand explicitly opts out — the growing calls from prominent banking officials to introduce legislation point to a bigger unresolved question: when an AI agent makes a purchase that a consumer later disputes, who is responsible for putting it right? For now, experts say much of that burden may still land with the retailer.

The news: OpenAI UK employees received £2.3 million on average in pay and shares in 2025.

Why it matters: OpenAI employees in the UK received an average of £2.3 million in pay and shares last year, according to newly filed UK accounts. The ChatGPT developer distributed £255 million in salary and equity compensation among 110 staff — more than double the previous year’s average package. The figures reflect fierce competition for specialist AI talent, although remuneration varies significantly between employees, with leading researchers commanding the largest pay.

It’s another sign that the AI bubble is far from bursting, with these salaries incentivizing employees for longer and reflecting the budgets of the venture capitalists funding the company. (OpenAI’s most recent funding round reached $122 billion in March 2026, at an $852 billion post-money valuation.)

OpenAI’s UK revenue more than tripled to £293 million in 2025, as London becomes an increasingly key international AI research and commercial hub.

The news: Pinterest launches AI-powered beauty guides.

Why it matters: Pinterest has launched Beauty Guides, an AI-powered feature that analyzes hair and nail Pins to generate personalized salon instructions, at-home tutorials and product recommendations. Available first to US iOS users, Pinterest says the tool aims to close the gap between discovering a beauty look and recreating it.

While competitors like Google Lens already use AI-powered visual search to identify and shop products, Pinterest is extending the concept to practical beauty advice. The move reflects a broader shift from AI-powered discovery to AI-assisted decision-making, while helping Pinterest turn its vast repository of visual inspiration into greater shopping intent and potential commercial opportunities for beauty brands. It also builds on the platform’s recent push into visual search advertising, giving it more ways to connect inspiration with purchases.

The news: OpenAI releases virtual try-on in ChatGPT.

Why it matters: OpenAI is introducing AI-powered virtual try-on and product-saving features to ChatGPT, deepening its push into fashion e-commerce. Users can upload photos of themselves to visualize how clothing and accessories might look on, while a new Favorites feature lets them save products and try-on images for later.

Powered by ChatGPT Images 2.5 (OpenAI’s latest image generator, released in September) the tools will roll out globally across consumer plans, with purchases still completed on merchants’ websites. The launch puts ChatGPT in more direct competition with visual shopping platforms such as Google and Pinterest, as AI assistants evolve from product discovery tools into personalized shopping destinations.

For fashion brands, it raises the prospect of ChatGPT becoming an increasingly influential touchpoint in the customer journey. Luxury brands have been hesitant to adopt virtual try-on on their own channels, largely because the tech hasn’t met their visual standards — but the latest advances in AI are rapidly improving the realism of these digital models.

The news: TikTok launches AI shopping assistant and one-click checkout.

Why it matters: TikTok has launched an AI-powered shopping assistant that provides personalized product guidance, answering questions about sizing, availability and delivery directly within the app. Alongside this, a new one-click checkout feature allows users to purchase directly from brands on their FYP, without leaving TikTok.

Developed in partnership with commerce and payment providers including Shopify, Salesforce and Stripe, the features mark an expansion beyond TikTok Shop’s existing marketplace model, more deeply embedding AI-assisted shopping and direct brand purchases into the social platform. Its aim is to create a more seamless journey from inspiration through discovery, to purchase.

The launch comes against a wider shift towards AI-powered, end-to-end shopping experiences, alongside Meta’s Muse AI agent, Pinterest’s growing shopping investment, and ChatGPT’s new virtual try-on capabilities. For brands, the challenge becomes how to surface in these AI products, where checkout is handled outside their own channels.

The news: Trump orders the US government to stop calling AI “AI”.

Why it matters: President Donald Trump has signed an executive order instructing the US government to replace “artificial intelligence” and “AI” with “super intelligence” and “SI” across all official communications and policy documents, arguing that the new terminology better reflects the tech’s rapidly advancing capabilities and economic potential. The language notably echoes Mark Zuckerberg’s vision of “personal super intelligence”, which he made central to Meta’s AI strategy last year.

The rebrand comes amid a much bigger debate over who should be responsible for keeping increasingly powerful AI systems in check. Ahead of signing the executive order, Trump convened some of the industry’s most powerful figures — including Meta’s Zuckerberg, xAI’s Elon Musk, Google’s Sundar Pichai, Nvidia’s Jensen Huang, OpenAI’s Greg Brockman, and Anthropic’s Dario Amodei — to sign a voluntary AI safety accord.

The accord puts the onus on companies to develop internal safeguards, subject their systems to independent audits and provide board-level oversight, in line with the Trump administration’s preferred “self-policing” versus a coordinated slowdown. Amodei has been among the most vocal advocates for slowing down, while OpenAI, Google DeepMind, Microsoft and xAI have backed calls for greater restraint.

The bosses at Anthropic, Google, xAI, Meta, OpenAI, and Nvidia all signed Trump’s voluntary accord.

The news: Oura delays IPO, citing market uncertainty.

Why it matters: Smart ring maker Oura has postponed its hotly anticipated IPO, citing “uncertainty in the IPO market” despite strong demand for its wearables offering. The company had been targeting a valuation of as much as $14 billion, and expected to raise as much as $2.2 billion in the public listing.

The timing points to a broader AI-driven shake-up in public markets. Investors are increasingly scrutinizing the vast cash being poured into AI infrastructure and lofty tech valuations, even as prospective mega-listings from AI companies such as Anthropic and OpenAI threaten to command an outsized share of investor attention and capital. Oura’s delay offers an early glimpse of the potential divide between the tech companies attracting extraordinary sums and those competing for what’s left.

Plus, as of last week, OpenAI announced that it won’t be joining the IPO rush this year. CEO Sam Altman has said going public now would be ill-advised given the safety work still ahead.

The news: Meta unveils kawaii-coded wearable pendant to house its Muse AI assistant.

Why it matters: Just when we thought Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg had finished announcing his slew of smart glasses and AI updates during the annual connect conference, there was “just one more thing”: another wearable device designed to house its new Muse AI agent, the Muse Charm. It’s a tamagotchi-esque, palm-sized totem on a looped cord designed to attach to keychains or fit into the wearer’s pocket.

The Muse Charm has a small screen to display the user’s selected Muse AI avatar. It’s unlocked via fingerprint and can be spoken into for instructing their agent to carry out tasks. Zuckerberg is still “finalizing” the components, but promised that the device will be ready to ship before the holidays.

The Muse Charm is Meta’s latest attempt at a new form factor designed to get its AI agent into the hands of as many consumers as possible. “We packed the whole muse experience, including the real-time voice and avatar stack into something that fits on a key chain and is always available to talk to,” Zuckerberg said. “So if you’re not wearing glasses, this is going to be by far the fastest way to talk to your Muse and show [it] what’s going on around you.”

It feeds into Zuckerberg’s promise of “personal superintelligence”, and may offer itself as a successor to the smartphone.

The news: OpenAI pauses training of new AI models, following further AI agent hacks.

Why it matters: ChatGPT maker OpenAI has paused training of its new AI models, just hours after disclosing that its AI agents had gone “rogue”. Over the summer, there were reportedly dozens of incidents where OpenAI models unexpectedly breached their system guardrails and compromised organizations’ security controls, including the US government’s.

In July, OpenAI revealed its autonomous AI agents, developed during safety trials, escaped their testing sandbox, accessed the internet, and independently hacked the startup Hugging Face back in July — the first example of an major AI-driven attack that moved faster than the humans trying to contain it.

“We have not been as fast as we would have liked, but we are trying to balance our desire for transparency with gaining a clear understanding from petabytes [1 million gigabytes] of agent activity logs [digital diaries of what the agents do], and working with impacted organizations,” CEO Sam Altman said in a post on X. “Hugging Face is still the most severe event we’ve seen.” He added that OpenAI will “be as transparent as can be”, while hinting that OpenAI’s agents had also discovered vulnerabilities in “other companies” like rivals, which he said would “be their call to disclose or not”.

OpenAI said it will resume training of its most capable AI models only when “confident that we have additional safeguards and alignment improvements in place, which we are working on now,” a spokesperson told Vogue Business. “People want to know AI is being developed safely, and that starts with what companies like ours do. This is not the first time we have hit pause to take such measures, nor do we expect it to be the last as capabilities continue to advance.”

The news: OpenAI and Anthropic staff question how to put AI slowdown pledges into practice.

Why it matters: Employees at OpenAI and Anthropic are questioning how their leaders’ recent calls to slow the development of increasingly powerful AI models will work in practice. According to the Financial Times, staff were blindsided by the proposals, which include bringing independent evaluators inside the AI labs to scrutinize models and safety practices, leaving them to grapple with questions around how much access outsiders will receive and IP protection.

The uncertainty highlights the gap between the growing consensus that safety measures need to keep pace with rapidly advancing AI models, as well as the practicalities of implementing those commitments. It also raises questions over whether last week’s unusual show of agreement between AI leaders will translate into meaningful change around model development and release.

The news: AI’s biggest rivals unite behind calls to slow development.

Why it matters: Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei is calling on the AI industry to slow the rate at which its most powerful models become more capable, warning that safety measures are struggling to keep pace. His proposals include embedding independent evaluators inside AI labs, greater coordination on safety standards, and international cooperation. Anthropic has committed to bringing independent evaluators inside the company, giving them employee-like access to scrutinize the safety of its models.

What’s more, some of Amodei’s biggest rivals are backing him. OpenAI CEO Sam Altman said the tech company would also introduce independent evaluators, while xAI’s Elon Musk endorsed Amodei’s call and Google DeepMind’s Demis Hassabis said the proposal was “heading in the right direction”. The rare consensus comes after a series of AI safety incidents and high-profile researcher resignations intensified concerns over increasingly autonomous systems.

What’s yet unclear is whether the industry’s newfound agreement will translate into a meaningful slowdown, or how the biggest AI companies reconcile it with the fierce commercial race to build more powerful models.

The news: Estée Lauder Companies partners with AI startup to boost brands in search.

Why it matters: Estée Lauder Companies (ELC) is partnering with AI marketing platform Profound to track and improve how its portfolio of beauty brands appears across generative AI platforms like ChatGPT and Gemini. It comes as more fashion and beauty groups invest in recalibrating their marketing strategies to prioritize generative engine optimization (GEO); ELC said the strategic partnership marks a move to “operationalize GEO and AI marketing at scale”.

Profound’s software will show ELC when — and how — its brands surface or become recommended by AI assistants, and identify opportunities to improve their visibility and accuracy. The beauty conglomerate will then use Profound’s agentic tools to optimize product pages, blogs, social media, and other components so that information is easier for LLMs to comprehend and pick up.

“The way people discover beauty is being rewritten, and we intend to shape that shift rather than react to it,” said ELC global chief digital and marketing officer Aude Gandon, adding that the sector is undergoing an “industry-wide transition”, as consumers increasingly use AI for product discovery and recommendations.

The news: Daydream adds Siri and camera roll search via Apple’s new iOS 27.

Why it matters: AI shopping platform Daydream is integrating deeper into the iPhone with the launch of two features built on Apple’s new iOS 27 tools. Users can now find shoppable matches for clothing in photos saved to their camera rolls, or ask Siri to search Daydream’s catalog using conversational prompts without opening the app. Daydream is one of the early co-developers of tools built with Apple’s latest on-device AI foundation models, using its new image-context capabilities.

Daydream says the launch gives it access to shopping intent at the point of inspiration, rather than requiring consumers to begin their search on a retailer or marketplace. The company now has more than 1.5 million shoppers, as well as 3 million products spanning 10,000 brands and 325 retailers.

The news: US court rules Pentagon’s blacklisting of Anthropic as unlawful.

Why it matters: Anthropic has won a significant legal battle against the US government, after a federal judge ruled that the Pentagon acted unlawfully when referring to the Claude maker as a national security supply chain risk. The dispute came after Anthropic refused to remove safeguards preventing its AI from being used for autonomous weapons and domestic mass surveillance, leading to federal agencies and military contractors boycotting any business with the company.

In her ruling, US District Judge Rita Lin found the government’s measures to be “illegal and baseless”, and amounted to unlawful retaliation. The Pentagon could appeal the decision.

The case reinforces growing tensions emerging around who decides what an AI system can and can’t be used for — a risk for businesses using enterprise AI. As fashion and beauty companies increasingly build workflows and consumer tools on models from Anthropic, OpenAI and Google, they are also inheriting the safeguards, use policies and geopolitical exposure of those providers.

The news: Amazon faces lawsuit over training AI models on video content from Twitch.

Why it matters: The class action lawsuit against Amazon has been brought on behalf of millions of Twitch streamers who allege the platform’s parent company, Amazon, used their content to train AI without permission or compensation. According to the BBC, reportedly 215 million hours of content was generated via the platform in the first few months of 2026 alone. While the use of these videos was only announced in August, allowing people to then opt-out of having their data used this way, it is unclear how long Amazon was harnessing this content.

The legal proceedings tie into concerns around the ownership of content that’s hosted on Big Tech-owned platforms, as well as its illicit commercial usage that leaves creators without compensation or rights. Twitch and Amazon are yet to comment on the proceedings.

The news: Alibaba raises £7.5 billion to invest in AI.

Why it matters: Chinese retail and tech giant Alibaba is issuing 710 million shares in Hong Kong at around £10.54 each, with expectations of raising roughly £7.5 billion. The plan for the proceeds is to develop its “full stack” AI capabilities, including new models, chips, and computing infrastructure.

The move comes days after Alibaba’s quarterly profits reportedly plunged 75% off of rising AI infrastructure spend, as the business aims to capitalize on the strong sentiment for tech companies in China’s stock market, offset its slowing e-commerce business, and power proprietary tech such as its Qwen AI models.

Alibaba has ramped up its AI investment since being blacklisted by the Pentagon in June, alongside other Chinese companies deemed as a national security risk to the US. The company is potentially looking to compete with Silicon Valley’s suite of AI makers, while shifting gears to tech to be able to maximize and support its fashion operations in-house.

Why it matters: On August 18, the trial commenced for a lawsuit filed in 2023, in which 29 US states claim Meta violated multiple federal and state laws protecting children. Beyond the potential billions of dollars Meta may have to pay out if found liable, the states are demanding the tech leader make changes to its Instagram and Facebook models, such as removing like counts and the “infinite scroll”.

If Meta loses, the way young people experience social media may be forced to change. Through features designed to keep consumers using the platforms as long and as often as possible, the states believes Meta “chose to exploit” young people to grow its user base and ultimately its business. The trial poses questions for brands who utilize these social platforms for monetization and brand-building purposes — in terms of both accountability, as well as having to recalibrate their partnerships with social media and their methods for reaching younger audiences.

The news: Phia faces fresh scrutiny over affiliate attribution.

Phia is rapidly becoming a case study in the governance risks surrounding AI-powered shopping agents and browser extensions. As luxury brands increasingly experiment with AI commerce assistants and affiliate-driven discovery, the controversy highlights the need for rigorous oversight of attribution, consent and partner incentives. It also raises broader questions about how AI shopping tools should earn commissions and the level of transparency brands should expect from technology partners across AI and affiliate marketing.

The news: Anthropic investors eye a record $2 trillion IPO.

Why it matters: Anthropic investors are modelling an IPO worth more than $2 trillion, which would make it the largest public listing in history if achieved. According to the Financial Times, expectations are being fueled by the Claude maker’s rapid enterprise revenue growth and confidence that demand for frontier AI models will continue to accelerate. Anthropic has not confirmed a target valuation and remains in its quiet period ahead of a potential October listing, meaning the figure reflects investor expectations rather than company guidance.

A $2 trillion listing would reinforce the dominance of a handful of AI infrastructure companies that increasingly underpin the tools luxury brands rely on. It also adds fresh momentum to the AI bubble, characterized by the sector’s frothy valuations, underscoring that investor appetite for the biggest commercial AI models remains strong, despite growing questions over sustainability and returns.

The news: Mark Zuckerberg releases AI manifesto, unveiling “open source” Meta AI model, Muse Glimmer.

Why it matters: Alongside the release of Muse Glimmer, a new lightweight version of Meta’s most powerful AI model, which is “open source” (freely available to outside developers), CEO Mark Zuckerberg wrote a 6,500-word consumer-facing manifesto titled The Future Is for Everyone: The Path to a Positive AI Future.

As well as pitching the company’s plan to release free AI models to “billions of people”, Zuckerberg’s essay positions the tech as a tool for creativity, entrepreneurship and self-expression rather than replacement. By outlining an optimistic vision of consumer AI alongside its open model strategy, the company is attempting to differentiate itself from rival companies like Anthropic and OpenAI, who have warned that AI is dangerous and should be tightly controlled.

In a direct comment on rivals’ AI strategies, Zuckerberg wrote: “Meta is the company primarily focused on building personal superintelligence for everyone. Most other labs are focused on building AI for companies, governments, or other institutions, so if those labs lead, then the balance of power will favor larger institutions over individuals.”

It’s a move that speaks to broader consumer fears of AI’s impact on livelihoods — both from an economic standpoint and in terms of creativity. Rather than framing AI around productivity or cost savings, Meta is increasingly positioning it as a creative collaborator.

The news: Daydream launches B2B arm for AI fashion search.

Why it matters: AI shopping startup Daydream is expanding beyond its consumer app with Powered by Daydream, a white-label platform allowing fashion brands to embed its conversational and visual AI search directly into their e-commerce sites. Launch partners include Staud, Alice + Olivia and Cult Mia, with more than 25 additional brands signed on, according to the startup.

It joins a growing cottage industry of startups promising to “AI-proof” brand websites, betting that retailers would rather rent AI capabilities than surrender customer relationships to a new generation of shopping apps. At the same time, as the market for consumer-facing AI shopping apps becomes increasingly crowded, with many still struggling to prove shoppers want a new place to begin their purchasing journeys, it’s a notable pivot.

The news: Salesforce invests in Brunello Cucinelli’s AI platform, Callimacus.

Why it matters: Solomei AI, the Brunello Cucinelli-owned tech company behind Callimacus, has entered into an investment agreement with CRM giant Salesforce. The AI-powered platform, which launched in January 2026 via the Brunello Cucinelli e-commerce site, aims to position itself as the next generation of pageless websites and applications — rather than guiding customers through a standardized site experience, Callimacus’s AI agents are designed to recognize each visitor’s intent and generate a personalized shopping navigation in real time.

The Salesforce funding will support the push for Callimacus to be adopted by businesses across Europe and North America, and, if the transaction goes through, pending authorization by the Presidency of the Council of Ministers, the CRM firm will join seed shareholders such as the Cucinelli family holding company, Foro delle Arti.

The Callimacus system aims to offer businesses a new way to engage customers in the agentic age, beyond static e-commerce sites. Its majority ownership by Italian luxury label Brunello is also significant, considering its founder’s long-term interest in AI, as well as its popularity among the Silicon Valley tech set — the growing relationship between tech and fashion is now well-documented.

The news: Prime Minister Andy Burnham appoints the UK’s first AI minister.

Why it matters: Andy Burnham, who was officially named Prime Minister on July 20, appointed Kanishka Narayan MP as the UK’s first AI minister in one of his initial moves. The decision represents the acknowledgement of AI as a vital policy challenge for the government, considering the risk to British jobs, health and safety concerns, as well as the uncertainty surrounding the impact the tech might have on economic structures. It also comes off the back of Burnham’s belief that nations have a limited window to take control of AI and shape its impact on the country. However, some say the move reflects Britain’s efforts to join the global AI race, attempting to catch up with the likes of the US and China.

The news: OpenAI’s new merch line draws less-than-enthusiastic response.

Why it matters: OpenAI has opened its Supply Co. merchandise line to the public, selling branded T-shirts, hoodies, caps and accessories that it describes as “physical expressions of research energy”. Previously reserved for employees, the collection borrows from the language of limited-edition fashion drops, complete with archival pieces and collectible ephemera.

The internet, however, is largely unconvinced. Across social media, many are questioning who the products are for and likening the launch to Silicon Valley’s latest attempt to become a lifestyle brand, with critics taking aim at everything from the pricing to the aesthetic.

It’s the latest in a string of attempts by Big Tech and AI companies to borrow cues from fashion — whether that be merch, Palantir’s workwear jackets, or Meta’s AI smart glasses — in an effort to transform themselves from software providers into cultural brands.

The news: Apple escalates dispute with OpenAI over ex-employees.

Why it matters: Apple has intensified its dispute with OpenAI by contacting dozens of former employees now working at the ChatGPT parent company, asking them to retain documents that could become evidence in an ongoing trade secrets case. The move follows the lawsuit Apple filed last week, which alleges confidential information relating to its future hardware projects may have been taken by former staff who later joined OpenAI’s expanding devices business. OpenAI has rejected the allegations, saying it neither sought nor benefited from Apple’s proprietary information.

The fierce competition for experienced hardware talent reflects a broader shift, as AI companies look beyond chatbots towards consumer devices. Both Apple and OpenAI are reportedly developing AI-powered smart glasses, a category that could take wearable AI mainstream.

Apple enters that race with decades of experience building consumer hardware and one of the world’s most recognizable design identities. Meanwhile, OpenAI is trying to establish those credentials more rapidly through its partnership with Jony Ive and the acquisition of his AI firm, io.

The news: Meta discontinues controversial Instagram AI feature for new AI image generator.

Meta’s wider Muse Image text-to-image model, developed by its Meta Superintelligence Labs division and integrated across Meta AI, is still in place. The model enables users to generate images from text prompts, edit existing photos, apply visual effects, create custom graphics and produce creative assets within Meta's apps ecosystem, including Instagram, WhatsApp and Facebook. It’s part of Meta’s broader strategy to embed generative AI directly into its consumer products, rather than offering it as a standalone creative tool.

The news: Meta tests always-on “super sensing” smart glasses, FT reports.

Why it matters: Meta is testing a new line of smart glasses that would continuously record audio while taking photos every few seconds, according to the Financial Times, which cited multiple people familiar with the matter. The idea behind the new glasses, according to the reports, is that wearers could then ask the Meta AI model that’s built into the glasses what they have seen or heard, or to recall part of their day.

The news: Phia shopping app accused of claiming unearned affiliate sales.

Why it matters: AI shopping app Phia is facing criticism after an investigation by Bloomberg found that it improperly collected affiliate commissions on online purchases. The claims center on the app’s browser extension, which is alleged to have replaced existing rival affiliate tracking links with its own during checkout, allowing it to receive credit for sales it may not have originated. Phia has said the behavior resulted from a technical issue that has since been resolved. Given that Phia’s business centers on affiliate revenue, the allegations throw the startup’s credibility into question. They’ve also prompted renewed scrutiny of affiliate tracking practices across AI-powered shopping tools.

Fashion brands increasingly rely on affiliates — including publishers, creators and shopping platforms — to influence purchasing decisions, with commissions tied to accurate attribution. If AI shopping assistants increasingly become another layer between consumers and retailers, questions around who deserves credit for a sale, and how that credit is assigned, will likely become more significant.

The news: Film studio Neon acquires rights for Sam Altman biopic Artificial.

Why it matters: Indie film studio Neon has closed a deal to acquire the rights to produce Artificial, Luca Guadagnino’s forthcoming $40 million drama about Sam Altman’s leadership of OpenAI, after Amazon unexpectedly abandoned the project despite nearing completion. It comes after Netflix, A24, Mubi, and Focus all chose not to back the film after viewing it at pre-sale screenings, per reports by Variety.

Variety also reported that, according to those who have seen the film — which is set during a tumultuous time at the AI company, when Altman was fired and then reinstated as CEO in late 2023 — Guadagnino’s portrayal of the executive is less than favorable.

The move reignites questions about Big Tech’s growing influence over culture: Amazon’s decision followed its multi-billion-dollar OpenAI partnership, prompting speculation over whether commercial relationships are beginning to shape creative output. The news follows Google’s $75 million investment in indie film studio A24, which experts say was a bet on its creative influence and cultural legitimacy.

The news: “Exuberant” AI spending could threaten the global economy, the Bank for International Settlements warns.

Why it matters: The Bank for International Settlements (BIS), often referred to as the central bank for central banks, has warned that Big Tech’s trillion-dollar AI spending spree risks ending in a prolonged investment bust, if the promised returns fail to materialize. In a report published on Sunday, the BIS compared today’s AI race with historical investment booms — from Britain’s railway expansion to the dotcom bubble — and argued that transformative technologies often attract more capital than commercial returns can ultimately justify.

The warning comes as the five largest hyperscaling tech companies — Amazon, Microsoft, Alphabet, Meta and Oracle — are expected to spend more than $1 trillion on AI infrastructure by the end of 2026.

It’s another reminder that the AI boom is becoming a macroeconomic story that extends beyond the technology sector. Should investment in AI fail to deliver the gains markets are betting on, the resulting slowdown in business confidence and consumer spending would weigh on the entire economy.

The news: L’Oréal and OpenAI announce partnership for AI in beauty product development, content creation, and product discovery.

Why it matters: L’Oréal Group has announced a strategic partnership with OpenAI, marking one of the beauty industry’s most significant moves yet into conversational commerce. Unveiled at the Vivatech conference in Paris, the partnership will explore applications across research, marketing and consumer discovery, while bringing L’Oreal’s brands into ChatGPT, including Maybelline New York. Maybelline will also be part of an experiment into the virtual try-on (VTO) of some beauty products by the two companies.

L’Oréal said it will also work with OpenAI to strengthen product discovery within ChatGPT across brands in the US, from Lancôme to Kérastase. Meanwhile, Skinceuticals, Cerave, and Garnier are all part of ChatGPT’s global ad pilot.

It’s another sign that large fashion and beauty groups are investing in AIO, optimizing for AI assistants that are rapidly emerging as the next discovery layer. Experts are split, however, on whether VTO in beauty is something consumers actually want — some respondents to our Vogue Business AI consumer perception survey said it’s a category where influencer recommendations and in-store product testing are preferred.

The news: UK bans access to social media platforms for under-16s.

Why it matters: The UK is banning social media for under-16s, effective next year. Prime Minister Keir Starmer said the restrictions will be modeled on those set by the Australian government last December, comprising a ban for the age group on platforms where the “sole and significant purpose” is to facilitate social interaction. This will include Facebook, Instagram, Snapchat, Reddit, TikTok, X, YouTube and Twitch, likely alongside other platforms such as dating apps and Bluesky. Pinterest, YouTube Kids, and messenger apps like Whatsapp are not included in the ban.

The UK is going a step further than Australia, however, banning all platforms from offering live streaming to under-16s and prohibiting strangers from making contact with the age group. Gaming platforms like Roblox and Discord — also not included in the blanket ban — will still need to abide by these rules. AI chatbots enabling romantic companionship will be banned for under-18s.

It’s the latest sign that the anti-social media movement is reaching a tipping point, as the push to limit children’s exposure to addictive algorithms and other potential online harms extends beyond activist interests into the mainstream. Brands and marketers will need to adapt their strategies for how they reach these consumers, which will likely result in a much more fragmented marketing space. Experts say multiplayer games like Roblox and Fortnite — where under-16s are socializing more — could become an appealing alternative for brands.

The news: Meta launches AI agents for business users in Whatsapp monetization push.

Why it matters: Meta has introduced AI-powered business tools on Whatsapp, launching a Meta business AI agent, designed to handle customer interactions end-to-end — from answering customer queries and booking appointments, to recommending products and closing sales, without the need of a human. It’s one of Meta’s first moves into commercial-facing operations, outside its core advertising business, and unlocks a huge revenue stream for the tech giant as it continues to invest millions in AI.

Many luxury brands already use Whatsapp as an informal clienteling channel, for sales associates to maintain a direct, ongoing relationship with high-value customers. Embedding AI into that environment could allow brands to scale this communication beyond the highest value customers, without the need for hiring human teams. It’s also another sign of Big Tech’s push into conversational commerce.

But as brands look to build closer relationships with customers, the move also positions Meta as the third-party infrastructure layer through which those interactions increasingly flow. What’s more, Whatsapp’s appeal has long been its perception as a more private, encrypted space, but the introduction of AI-generated interactions could shift consumer perception of the space — particularly relevant for the luxury customers who value discretion and trust.

The news: Elon Musk’s SpaceX seeks to raise the world’s largest ever IPO.

Why it matters: Elon Musk’s space and AI company SpaceX is seeking to raise as much as $86 billion at a valuation of around $1.8 trillion, in what would be the world’s largest ever IPO, according to an amended IPO filing submitted last Wednesday.
